to probuce cycloidical curve"
The Sumitono Machinery Works Ltd., Niihama, Ehimeken, Japan,

following barg in with the Cyclo Company Ltd. And in thi

1. Cyclo transfers to Sumitomo an exclusive licence by the
above patent to manufacture and sell cyclo gears.

Manchuria.

gears produce! in Japan to China, and at the same time.

as Sumitomo keeps this right, Cyclo would never give the
said right in china to the third party.

region mentioned in the contract, and at the same time,
Cyclo owes the duty never to make direct sale to Japan and
Manchuria, both parties should pay five times of the selling
prices - R.M. 1,ooo as a minimum Limit _ as penalties, when
they breachei the above rule. This rule also binds the
selling agencies, which belong to both parties respectively
and act in their places.

rect sale outsile the re ion in the contract.
5. Sumitomo owes the luty not to give sub-licence in the
region in the contract.

Sumitomo and Sumitomo obtains from Cyclo, ome set of
special machines, as follows:

MEMORANDUM FOR: IMPERIAL JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
THROUGH:
Central Liaison Office, Tokyo
SUBJECT:
Tranafer of Zaibatsu Family Properties to Holding
Company Liquidation Commission
1. References are the following Memoranda for the Imperial Japanese
Government from General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers:
a. F1le AG 004 (6 Nov 45)ESS/ADM, (SCAPIN 244) dated 6 November
1945, subject: Dissolution of Holding Companies.
b. File AG 319.1 (19 Jan 46)ESS/AG, (SCAPIN 625) dated 19 January
1946, subject: Reports to be Made by Certain Business Firms.
c. File AG 004 (4 Apr 46) ESS/AC, (SCAPIN 859), dated 4 Apr 1946,
subject: Holding Company Liquidation Commission.
d. File AG 091.4 (3 Jun 46)ESS/AC, (SCAPIN 995), dated 3 June 1946,
subject: Reports to be made by Certain Families.
e. File AG 091.3 (23 Jul 46)ESS/AC, (SCAPIN 1079) dated 23 July
1946, subject: Ordinances and Regulations Affecting the Holding Company
Liquidation Commission.
2. The Imperial Japanese Government is directed to take such action
as may be necessary within five (5) deys from the date of this Memorandum,
to enlarge the jurisdiction of the Holding Company Liquidation Commission
and to vest in it express authority to receive, take over, hold, manage
and administer the property and assets, of whatever description, individu-
ally, jointly, or otherwise held, of designated families or family
members listed in paragraph 3, below. The Holding Company Liquidation
Commission shall exercise the authority so granted with a view toward
eventually liquidating, distributing and compensating much family members
for assets and property taken over and in an equitable and democratic
manner consistent with the objectives stated in Memoranda for the
Imperial Japanese Government from General Headquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, referred to in paragraphs la and e, above.
3. "Designeted families or family members" refers to the follow-
ing families whose activities have heretofore been restricted by
Memoranda

4. The Imperial Japanese Government is directed to take whatever
action may be necessary to transfer to the Holding Company Liquidation
Commission the functions now performed by the Ministry of Finance
pursuant to the provisions of Memoranda to the Imperial Japanese Government
from General Headquar ters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
referred to in paragraphs la and d and part of the Memorandum referred
to in paragrah lb, above, including the powers and responsibilities of
the Ministry of Finance thereunder, together with its files, records,
information and other date relating thereto, insofar as they have pertained
to the designated families and family members listed in paragraph 3, to the
end that it shall be further relieved of such functions and the Holding
Company Liquidation Commission fully charged therewith.
5. Direct communication is authorized between the interested Staff
Sections of General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
and agencies of the Imperial Japanese Government concerned to implement
all provisions of this Memorandum.

Enclosure to Despatch No. 1384
CENTRAL NEWS AGENCY
ENGLISH SERVICE
BACKBONE OF ZAIBATSU CONTROL SAID STILL INTACT
Tokyo, June 18 (Central): The Zaibatsu influence, which enabled
12 families to manipulate 80 per cent of Japan's commerce, industry
and finance, is still very much alive, though SCAP announced recently
that the "super-structure of Zaibatsu control has been completely
demolished."
Approaching the subject from three angles, informed sources
contacted by Central News said that one can easily see that damage
has been done only to the tentacles of the Zaibatsu octopus. Its
nerve center has been left pretty much intact.
Personally and organizationally, though the holding companies
are being liquidated, almost all their daughter companies are carry-
ing on business as usual as indepedent companies under the supervision
of mn thoroughly steeped in Zaibatsu traditions. Zaibatsu leaders
may have been purged, but their yesmen and righthand men still are
free, ready to do the biddings of their former bosses.
Sources contacted by Central News said that there are plenty
of indications that a considerable amount of Zaibatsu money is
being pumped into blackmarket activites to make handsome profit.
The same source continued that to all intents and purposes,
Zaibatsu today is still controlling the country's commerce, in-
dustry and fihance. He emphasized that none of the big Zaibatsu
banks have been reorganized. They are functioning as well as
supporting various "independet companies" formerly affiliated with
Zaibatsu.
Also under the anti-trust law, each shareholder is allowed
to hold 10 percent of the total shares of the company. There is
no preventing to the shareholder getting more shares under various
names. When the holding company liquidation commission starts to
sell out shares, under the same law, the employees of the various
"independent companies" will be given priority in purchasing them.
Then all the proceeds from the shares after payment of the property
taxes will be turned over to Zaibatsu by the commission.
Thus, the major portion of the former Zabatsu capital will
still be concentrated in Zaibatsu hands, the same source said.
"Far more drastic and thorough-going measures are needed to break
the backbone of the Zaibatsu control", he said.
According to a SCAP release of June 12, stock securities of
67 holding companies have been transferred to the holding company
liquidation commission. These holding companies had 3,658,000
subsidiaries and affiliated concerns.
The SCaP release added that 2,210,000 Zaibatsu executives
have been removed from 1632 companies.

Office Memorandum · UNITED STATE
:A-8/M. t-
TO
DATE: November S,
FROM : A-S/Mr. Rudlin L
sUBJECT: Japanese Deconcentration Ordinance and SWNCC 302/2.
Pursuant to arrangements made by you and Mr. Draper on
October 29th, Messrs. Whitman, Vernon, Owen and Rudlin met
October 3lst with Assistant Secretary Gray and Messrs. Fahy,
Pollard and Stevens of the Civil Affairs Division, to discuss
the above subject.
The chief points brought out were as follows:
1. A later draft of the Ordinance has been received and
according to working levels in CAD is considerably loss objection-
able than its predecessor.
2. The five points relating to the Ordinance which Mr. Draper
informed you were disturbing Secretary Royall, are as follows:
894.602
(a) Inadequate provision for just and fair compensa-
tion to those affected by the Zaibatsu program;
(b) The danger of breaking up concentrations into
units so amall as to be unworkable;
(c)
Fears regarding the "patent situation". We were
unable to elicit anything speoific here beyond
-347
the fact that SWNCC 302/2 directs SCAP to do
certain things regarding patents on a mandatory
rather than a permissive basis;
(d) Inadequate provision for judicial review and
excessive delegation of legislative powers;
(e)
Fear that the HCLC could go beyond the basis
policy of breaking up large concentrations (with
which policy Mr. Grey stated the Army Department
was in full agreement) to the point of taking
action against almost any firm.
3. After a good deal of discussion, I suggested, and Mr. Gray
agreed, that in essence the Army Department's concern was two-fold:
they are fearful that the implementation of this particular reform
program will interfere with economic recovery and, ewen if it does
not

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.97)
-8
not, that the means adopted may appear radical, un-American, and
hard to defend before the Congress.
It was agreed that SCAP should be told yes or no on the
Ordinance as soon as possible, without waiting for full reconsidere-
tion of SWNCC 302/2. State and Army are studying the new version
of the Ordinance and will meet again November 5th to draft a cable.
In the meantime, Army will firm up its comments on SWNCC 302/2.

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
T.
TO
: A-S Mr. Se1tzman
DATE: November 12, 1949
FROM : A-S Mr. Rudlin
sUBJECT: Possible Queries Regarding FEC 230.
1. Just Compensation - if this is raised, our reply is as follows. We
have admitted the principle of compensation in the Zaibatsu Program. The basic
purpose is to divest a group of its economic resources and political power. This
can not be done by giving the Zaibatsu their properties back in the form of yen
or government bonds. It is doubtful if slavery would have been abolished when
it was if Lincoln had to work out ade uate compensation schemes before Emancipation.
This has been tried in Jepan in the time of the eiji Restoration when the large
feudel families were bought out pclitically and economically by the government.
It did not take them long to buy themselves back in again. This is the answer
if Secretary Royall makes the point that compensation based on the purchase price
of li uidsted securities should be supplemented by government contributions. If
Secretery Royall should feel that government subsidy is undesirable, the only
alternative is for the HCIC to hold the shares indefinitely in the hope of a higher
price. This would seriously delay and inhibit economic recovery and make it even
harder to obtain a good price for the securities.
It should also be pointed out that the Zaibatsu program has come to have for
many Japanese a symbolic significance which goes far veyond the economics of the
matter. ror them, the abolition or the Zaibatsu system is the biggest step that
could be taken towara a peaceful and democratic Japan. For us to insist upon fair
or just compensation and thereby rob the progrem of its meaning and effect would
persuade many Japanese that there is something in the Soviet argument that the
U.S. is more capitalist than democratic.
2. In general American labor unions are exempted by the Clayton Act from the
2
monopoly and anti-trust laws except where a union conspires with some organization
other than a union to restrain trade. The exemption in the case of Japan is based
also upon the fact that there has never been any union movement of consequence
in Japanese history and it was thought advisable to encourage almost non-existent
labor organizations rather than to enjoin them from doing things which they were
unlikely to attempt. However, if the Army Department has specific reason to believe
that labor unions or cooperatives are "going too far" then we should ask SCAP for
a report and, if that bears out the Army Department's fears, we can issue the
appropriate directives.
3. The Report of the Edwards Mission was made in May 1946 to the War and
State Departments via SCAP.
11
3604

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
STATBCENT OF U. S. POLICY TOWARD BOONOHIC RECOVERY OF JAPAN
1. Part I--Ultimate Objectives--of the Basic Post-Surrender Miey for J
as approved by the Far Eastern Commission, is as follows:
"1. The ultimate objectives in relation to Japan, to which policies for the
post-surrender period for Japan should conform are:
"a. To insure that Japan will not again become a menace to the peace
and security of the world.
"b. To bring about the earliest possible establishment of a democratie
and peaceful government which will carry out its international responsi-
bilities, respect the rights of other states, and support the objectives
of the United Nations. Such government in Japan should be established in
accordance with the freely expressed will of the Japanese people.
"2. These objectives will be achieved by the following principal means:
"a. Japan's sovereignty will be limited to the islands of Honshi,
Hokkado, Kyushu, Shikoku and such minor outlying islands as may be determined.
"b. Japan will be completely disarmed and demilitarised. The authority
of the militarists and the influence of militarim will be totally eliminated.
All institutions expressive of the spirit of militarism and aggressions will
be vigorously suppressed.
"c. The Japanese people shall be encouraged to develop a desire for
individual liberties and respect for fundamental human rights, particularly
the freedoms of religions, assembly and association, speech and the press.
They shall be encouraged to form a democratic and representative organizations.
"d. Japan shall be permitted to maintain such industries as will sustain
her economy and permit the exaction of just reparations in kind, but not
those which would enable her to rearm for war. To this end, macess to, as
distinguished from, control of, raw materials should be permitted. Eventual
Japanese participation in world trade relations will be permitted."
2. The U.S. Government has reviewed the accomplishments of the first two
years of the occupation of Japan in the light of the ultimate allied objectives as
listed in the Basic Post-Surrender Policy for Japan. This review has revealed that
the SCAP has taken the steps necessary to assure the accomplishment of the objective
SECRT
1

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
that Japan shall not again menace the peace and security of the world. In imple-
mentation of the basic policy the SCAP has destroyed Japan's ability to make war
the land, on the sea, and in the air, and has eliminated the exponents of Japanese
militarim and ultra-nationalism from positions of power in Japan. Under the
surveillance of the Allied Powers and later mrreiller
Japan should not again menace the peace and security of the world.
3. Great strides have also been made toward bringing about conditions whereby
a democratic and peaceful Japan can assume the responsibilities of a respected
member of the community of nations in accordance with the second ultimate objective
of the Basic Post-Surrender Policy. A peaceful and democratic Japanese Government
has been established in accordance with the provisions of an enlightened consti-
tution adopted by the freely expressed will of the Japanese people. The ravages of
the war and the penalties which the Japanese people are still paying for blindly
following their leaders in a war of aggression are such, however, that Japan does
not yet have the economic strength and stability to maintain a self-sufficient
economy and to carry out all of its international responsibilities. At present
economic stability in Japan is maintained at the expense of the American people
through the importation of vital food and other materials required to prevent wide-
apread disease and unrest.
4. The first thrae of the four principal means of achieving the ultimate allied
objectives regarding Japan, as listed in the Basic Post-Surrender policy. has been
fully utilized. The authority of the Japanese Government is now limited to the four
main islands and a few minor outlying islands. Japan has been completely disarmed
and demilitarized. The authority of the militarists has been eliminated and the
Japanese people have been encouraged to develop a desire for individual liberties
and respect for fundamental rights.
5. With regard to the fourth of these principal means, Japanese industry and
commerce are not yet sufficient to sustain the Japanese economy, there is not yet
final Allied determination of the reparations Japan will be required to pay, and
Japan is not yet in a position to participate fully in world trade and to contribute
her indispensable part to the economic rehabilitation of the Far East.
6. It is now apparent that the first phase of the occupation has been completed
SECRET
2

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
Poliey and to establish the conditions requisite for a peace treaty that will
enable Japan to take her rightful place in the community of nations, the ecomomie
recovery of Japan for her benefit and that of the entire Jar Eastern aree mmst
hereafter be stressed.
7. It is realized that this is not fully attainable until thore is Alliod
determination of (1) the capital plant and equipment to be retained for the Japanese
economy and (2) the capital plant and equipment to be delivered by Japan as repare-
tions. Expert studies, which should form the basis for this determination, will
soon be completed. The U.S. Government will then endeavor to obtain early Allied
agreement regarding this vital matter.
8. Meanwhile, all possible efforts must be made to reduce, as soon as possible,
the drain on U.S. resources resulting from the cost of sustaining the Japanese
economy. Pending determination of a Japanese level of industry and Japanese repare
tions the U.S. Government will, therefore, take all possible and necessary steps,
within the limits of its authority, to encourage by all appropriate means and to the
maximum extent possible consistent with the basic objectives of the occupation, the
ability of Japan to support heroelf.
9. The U.S. Government accordingly considers that the Japanese Government must
devise and develop plans under SCAP supervision for the economic recovery of Japan
to the end that the Japanese economy will be balanced at the earliest possible time.
Greater efforts by the Japanese people, coupled with such assistance as the American
Government may be able to provide for a temporary period, should eliminate the burden
on the U.S. taxpayer of supporting the Japanese economy. Not only is a self-
sustaining Japanese economy necessary to the early achievement of political stability
in Japan, but the U.S. people cannot be expected indefinitely to subsidize the
economy of Japan. It will accordingly be one of the principal U.S. objectives to
bring about in Japan a self-supporting economy as soon as possible as one means of
aiding in the democratization of Japan and to eliminate the need for contimued
American financial support.
10. It is therefore the policy of the U.S. Government that existing directives
to SCAP should be interpreted and implemented by the appropriate shift of emphasis
so as to facilitate the early revival of the Japanese economy on a peaceful. self-
supporting and democratic basis.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: November 21, 1947
SUBJECT: Proposal to reconsider Zaibatsu Program.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. John J. McCloy, President,
International Bank for Reconstruction
and Development
A-S, Mr. Wisner.
COPIES TO:
U - Mr. Lovett
894.602
ese
Pursuant to instructions of Mr. Lovett, Mr. Wisner called upon Mr.
McCloy at 10:30  a.m.November 21 and showed him the memorandum dated Novem-
ber 19, 1947 which was prepared for Secretary Marshall. Mr. MoCloy read
the memorandum carefully and also examined the attachments. Of the attech-
11-2147
ments, he read most carefully SCAP cable dated October 28 (Tab C).
Following his reading of the memorandum and other items, Mr. MoCloy
made statements to the following effect:
1. That he personally had been involved in the preparation of
the basic deconcentration policy and in fact had a largo part in the
drafting of SCAP's directive which contains the general but very
positive provision directing the dissolution of excessive combine-
tions of economic power. This directive was fully considered prior
to its promulgation by the Secretaries of the Departments concerned,
including particularly War, and also received the careful personal
consideration of the President. Moreover, it was flown out to
General MacArthur, who read and approved the proposed directive prior
to its issuance.
Some program for the dissolution of the Zaibatsu was a part of
the broad policy which was considered necessary in order to effect
the democratization of Japan. It was not primarily intended as a
punitive measure in connection with responsibility for war guilt,
since
SECRET

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2
since there was considerable doubt, at least in the mind of Mr.
Stimson, that Zaibatsu elements had actively supported the war party
in Japan.
Mr. McCloy commented upon the MacArthur cable of October 28,
generally to the effect that notwithstanding its empurpled passages,
it makes out a very strong case for MacArthur's position.
2. Mr. McCloy does not believe that he has ever seen, and is
quite certain that he had no hand in the preparation or approval of
any ordinance or other document providing for the specific implemen-
tation of the policy. When he was in Japan in October 1945, Mr.
McCloy had some conversations with an attorney on the staff of General
MacArthur who had sketched out orally his intentions with regard to
expropriation and deconcentration. These proposals, which were rather
aggressively put forward, contained certain aspects which impressed Mr.
McCloy as being quite drastic, and he advised the proponent, and
later General MacArthur, that these proposals involved questions of
policy which by all means should be approved in Washington. General
MacArthur was at first disposed to argue this point, but later came
around to apparent agreement, and was regeptive to Mr. McCloy's sug-
gestion that experts from the Department of Justice, and possibly
from Treasury, should be sent out to assist General MacArthur in the
formulation of appropriate legal procedures. Mr. McCloy belioves that
it was this suggestion which resulted in the Corwin Edwards mission to
Japan. (This mission, which was composed of lawyers and economic and
financial experts, spent about three months in Japan making an inten-
sive study of the situation and reporting their conclusions to SCAP,
and via SCAP to Washington.) This latter development occurred sub-
sequent to Mr. McCloy's departure from office, and he is unfamiliar
with the findings and conclusions of the report.
3. Regarding the soundness of the basic principle and the ad-
visability of submitting it for review to Mr. Stimson or a committee
or group. to be composed of Mr. Stimson and certain other prominent
citizens, Mr. McCloy made the following observations: Mor the reason
stated in paragraph (1) above, the basic policy has already received
ample consideration at the highest level. Moreover, it is now too
late to reverse the basic policy. The general character of this policy
and the fact that it will be enforced in some manner has long been
widely known in Japan, and is by this time quite fully discounted. To
reverse the policy now would upset SCAp's administration in Japan and
would invite widespread criticism in this country - "by no means alone
from such organs as PM".
Moreover, it would be a very difficult assignment to thrust upon
Mr. Stimson and one which might ultimately involve him in sharp contro-
versy with General MacArthur. Mr. Stimson is accustomed to working in
a special way, and it has been his habit and practice in the past to
rely heavily upon a staff of competent advisers and assistants. He
has
SECRET

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-3-
has no such staff about him now, and would find it very diffioult to
do this kind of work alone. Mr. Stimson has requested Mr. McCloy's
advice about taking on this job, and Mr. McCloy has indicated that he
believes it would be contrary to Mr. Stimson's own personal interests
to do so at this time.
4. If, however, the question involved here is one of reviewing
the proposed mechanics of implementation, this is a matter which can
certainly be dealt with by such persons as Mr. Royall, Mr. Lovett,
Mr. Saltzman and their staffs who are fully competent to survey and
make recommendations with regard to the execution procedures. More-
over they are the proper persons to do this job. If they feel that
they require expert advice of outsiders, they should call upon experts
to assist them. It would be unnecessary and unwise to call upon Mr.
Stimson or a group of outstanding citizens to undertake the burden of
a complete review of the implementation procedure. If, after the
procedure had been fully reviewed and threshed out by the responsible
governmental officials, they should feel that they would like to obtain
the opinion of an outside group of "semi-statesmen", this might be
feasible.
Mr. McCloy concluded by offering to be of such further assistance
as he might be called upon to render.
Frank SWisner

DRAFT CABLE FROM WAR TO SCAP
November 30, 1947
CINCFE,
TOKYO, Japan
Provided following changes or their equivalent are made in the bill
on the dissolution of concentrations of economic power there is no further
objection here to its passage or implomentation.
(A) Add the word "excessive" wherever the word "ooncentration" appears
throughout the bill.
(B) Article 2. Change the last sentence of definition of "enterprise
of monopolistio characteristios" to read as follows: "in this case 'influence'
shall mean control sufficient to have a substantial effect upon the price or
flow of capital or upon the price or flow of goods or servioes, if the
enterprise were withdrawn from the market".
(C) Article 3. Omit from preamble "anumerated below" and add at end of
preamble the following sentence: "Buch excessive concentrations shall be
designated from among the enterprises or concentrations enumerated below,
upon the basis of standards developed in accordance with Article 6".
(D) Article 8. Add sentence to second paregraph reading as follows:
"The written findings shall disclose in detail the economio, manufaoturing,
and other data upon which the order is based, or such information shall be
transmitted with the order in memorandum form in addition to the findings."
(E) Article 13.
SFCRET

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
SECRET
(z) Article 13. Change the words "6 days" to "30 daya".
tr) Article 14. Change the words "15 days" to "30 days".
In view of the extreme importance of policy and standards r eliminatien
of excessive concentrations under the proposed law, it is suggested that
the standards to be promilgated under Srticle 6 should be prepared on an
industry by industry basis rather than in general terms applicable to the

1. I have the honor to transmit a clipping of
an editorial article appearing in the December 10, 1947,
issue of The Times on the subject of the dissolution of
the Zaibabau Industrial combinations in Japan.
2. The Times considers that relaxation of the
present allled attitude toward the Zaibatsu "would
profoundly affect Japanese domestic politics". If the
Zaibatsu were granted an amnesty in whatever form, The
Times comments, "the foroes of old Japan would be
Immensely strengthened". While admitting that the
eclipse of men trained to exercise economic power has
Y
made itself felt, The Times feels that they oan be
gradually replaced "by others accepting popular govern-
ment as the foundation of Japan's future"

Struggle in Japan
Messages fromWashington reveal
doubts about the practicability of
GeneraL MacArthur's plan to break up
the great industrial combinations con-
trolled by the Zaibatsu groups. In part
these doubts spring from the fear that no
one in Japan is capable of replacing the
Zaibatsu, and that a collapse of Japanese
economy would impose new and for-
midable burdens upon American tax-
payers. Additional uneasiness comes
from the leakage of a confidential docu-
ment known as F.E.C.230, presented to
the Far Eastern Commission last May by
the State Department. This document
proposed the sale of large Japanese busi-
nesses to new owners, some of whom
might be Japanese trade unions, most of
them under Communist control. The
socialization of important Japanese indus-
trial enterprises would be highly dis-
tasteful to many people in the United
States, especially if brought about by
American agency. For the moment
F.E.C.230 has been withdrawn, but the
policy of making Japan a country of
small-scale businesses has not so far been
changed.
Relaxation of the present attitude of the
Allied Command towards the Zaibatsu
would profoundly affect Japanese domes-
tic politics. On the basis of the law against
cartels passed by the Diet in October,
1946, a special division at allied head-
quarters has set out to destroy the power
of the Zaibatsu and to purge their leaders.
Most of the great industrial families have
been driven out of business. Their politi-
cal and economic influence has not yet
been broken, but it must work under-
ground, through puppets, and has less
effect. The conservative elements of
society, in whose hands power has hitherto
finally rested, are much handicapped in
the endeavour to build up an " invisible
"Government" ready to overthrow the
new men brought up by democratic pro-
cesses and to take over control when the
allied occupation comes to an end. Covert
opposition of this kind is now mobilizing
discontent against the Katayama Cabinet,
and if the Zaibatsu were granted an
amnesty, in whatever form, the forces of
"old Japan" would be immensely
strengthened. The middle class lacks
authority in Japan and is weak in leader-
ship. The eclipse of the men trained to
exercise economic power made itself
seriously felt this autumn, when the re-
establishment of foreign banks, the re-
opening of foreign shipping offices, and
the restoration of limited facilities for
foreign trade renewed, however tenta-
tively, Japanese contacts with the outside
world. These trained men can no doubt
be replaced as time goes on by others
accepting popular government as the
foundation of Japan's future, and to
restore them to power now would be to
endanger the main results of the allied
occupation for the sake of immediate
economic stability. Stability is out of
reach until a peace settlement with the
allies has defined the place Japan
will occupy in international commerce.
General MacArthur sees this as clearly
as any man. He has protested energeti-
cally against any suspension of the canay
psian against the Zaibath

Acting Secretary of State
MR. SALTZMAN
Washington, D. C.
Dear Bob:
In accordance with your request, I have had pre-
pared two papers relative to SWNCC 302/2. One paper
sets forth specific objections which we have found to
SWNCC 302/2 as it is now written. The other is a pro-
posed recast of SWNCC 302/2. I have asked Assistant
Secretary of the Army Gray to forward these papers to
Mr. Wisner. While I have not had an opportunity to
study them, I thought it well to have them sent over
68
as a basis from which we might work. I am informed
that they have been given careful consideration in the
working levels of this Department.
2
In connection with the Japanese economic situa-
tion, I feel very strongly that it is essential that
12-1647
we send some top flight man to Japan to take a look
at the picture and to come to some conclusion as to
whether there is indicated the necessity for a change
in direction or course. It seems to me impossible
for us in Washington to determine the extent to which
the basic objectives of the occupation have been ac-
complished, and also the extremes to which SCAP should
go in further implementing the program as directed by
the President, the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and the Far
Eastern Commission.
I have in mind such men as George L. Harrison,
President, New York Life Insurance Co., and Thomas B.
McCabe, President, Scott Paper Co. If someone is sent
to Japan, he should be a man of real stature and in
addition, should be armed with authority. I also feel
that time is important, and that we should move as
rapidly as possible.
SFCRET
4078

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.95)
SECRET
Such a re-examination seems to be imperative, in
view of the fact that in the last two years there have
been profound changes in the world situation, and in
the attitude of the American people toward the economic
circumstances of the occupied areas.
I should appreciate your reaction to this proposal.
Sincerely yours,
My
Kenneth C. Royall
Secretary of the Army

release contains a statement by Mr. Edward C. Welsh, Chief of the
Anti-Trust and Cartels Division of the Economic and Scientifie
Section of this Headquarters, in which he states that closure and
dissolution of the Association will permit the rise of democratie
trade organisations which will be encouraged to raise the ethical
standards of the construction industry, perform useful researeh and
investigation and represent the industry before the public.

The General Contractors' Association will be·de
closed institution by March 1, 1948, SCAP's Economic

nation's construction industry.

to fill the great need existing for bona fide trade association
in the construction industry to assist in raising its ethical
standards, rerform useful research and investigation, and to

TO
: Mr. Wisner
DATE: December 26, 1947
FROM : Mr. Rudlin 
sUBJeCT: Review of Zaibatsu Policy Paper -- SWNCC SO/2 (FEC 230)
The following papers are attached:
1. SWNCC 2C2/2
2.
FEC 230
3.
Army Department working level revision of SWNCC 302/2
4.
State Department working level revision of SWNCC 302/2
cleared through IR OE NA and currently under consideration
in FE and A-S.
In considering the above papers you may wish to bear in mind the
following points. The basic intent of the changes made by the Army Department
appears acceptable to the State Deptartment. The difference between the
Army and State versions can be accounted for, in general, by the following
b
considerations. First, the Army has in some places abbreviated the original
paper to the point where it would lose some of its necessary meaning. Second,
为
there are other places in the Army draft which do not appear to alter the
meaning of the original and it is felt by State thet the language of the paper
9
should be changed only to the extent necessary to remove undesirable meaning
0
or detail. Third, the Army apparently considers that certain  policies
2
proposed in the original paper are not strictly relevant to its subject, e.g.
those relating to corporate practices. State feels that such matters are
an integral part of deconcentration policy in Japan and should, therefore,
be covered in this paper.
None of the above points are major or substantive and it should be
possible to resolve them in conferencen between the two Departments.
You will note that Section 23 of the State revision, "Relationship of Execu
tion of this Policy to Japenese Economic Recovery" does not appear in either
the original paper or in the Army revision. It was specifically included by
Stete in order to meet one of the problems with which we believe top Army levels
and other persons to be most seriously concerned, namely, the relationship
between the execution of the policy and economic recovery in Japan.

Mr. Mitman
sUBJECT: Implemer tation of EC-230 in Japan
1. At your request, a study has becn conducted to determine:
a. to what extent both the old and new versions of FEC-230
have been implemeted in Japan;
b. to vhat extent recent Japanese legislation concerning
deconcentration is consistent with both the old and new versions
of FHC-230.
This study has been concluded. Its results are summarised
below; a ore detailed presentation of these results will be
forwarded to your office shortly.
2. In re implementation of FEC-230:
(a) a good deal of the legislation has been passed, the larger
holdin companies are in an adve ced stage of dissolution, and the
securities ovmed by major Zaibatsu families have been vested by the
goternment;
(b) certain vital legislation remains to be passed (notably
that relatin to the decoucentration of the control of credit). the
Zaibatsu securities remain to be liquidated, and the enterprises
other than holding companies which ere designated as excessive con-
1-1948
centrations remein to be dissolved.
It should be borne in mind that the legislation so far passed
is very general in. language, and does little more than grant to the
Japanese Covernment authority to carry out the program in question.
The passage of this legislation shoula thus not be considered as a
major implementing step, or as in any way fulfilling the requirements
of FC-230.
The statements made above ere applicable to both versions of
FEC-230, since, although the new paper is much shorter, it contemplates
a program of the same general scope as the old paper.
3. Japanese legislation concerning deconcentration would appear to
be consistent with the revised FEC-230. This legislation falls somewhat
short of meeting the standards spccified in the original FEC-230 and in the
Report of the ission in Japanese Combines from which the original FEC-230
was derived.

JAPANESE LEGISLATION AND ACTIONS IMPLEMENTING FEC-2SO
All article and paragraph references in this report are to specifie
articles and paragraphs of FEC-230, Policy on Exoessive Concentrations of
Economic Power in Japan, May 12, 1947. The revision of FEC-230 now being
considered by the US is in general, a summarization of the more detailed
policy specified in FEC-230; therefore, the comments below are also appli-
cable to this revision.
Article l. Objective
The statement of objectives is a summary of specifio items covered
in succeeding articles discussed below.
Article 2. Definition of an Excessive Concentration
First paragraph. This definition is used almost verbatim in Artiole
3 of the Elimination of Excessive Concentration of Economio Power Law (here-
after called Excessive Concentration Law) passed by the Japanese Diet on
December 9, 1947.
Second and Third Paragraphs. The same general criteria are •stablished
in the Japanese Excessive Concentration Law, with specific standards for
designation of an excessive concentration left to the discretion of the Hold-
ing Company Liquidation Commission (HCLC). The concept of presumption of
existence of an excessive concentration "subject to refutation" is not as
severely stated in the Japanese law as it is in FEC-230.
Fourth Pagagraph. Larger Japanese enterprises have been surveyed as
a result of SCAP directives and Japanese Ordinances, and a Sohedule of
Restricted Concerns has been set up. Final orders affecting deconcentration
are to be determined under the Excessive Concentration Law. At this stage
of development it cannot be determined whether or not the FEC-230 instruction
that "uncertainty as to whether any specified enterprise is covered by these
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 123 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
should
standards/be resolved in favor of coverage" is being carried out in
determining whioh concentrations are excessive.
Article 3. Dissolution vs Non-Dissolution of Exoessive Concentrations
Immediate dissolutan of excessive concentrations, as called for
in FEC-230, has not been implemented in Japanese legislation, but the
Japanese Excessive Concentration Law states as its aim the elimination of
excessive concentrations at "the earliest possible date" (Artiole 1).
Provision is made in the Japanese Excessive Concentration Law (Article
6) for consideration of the effects of a change in organizational struoture
of a company on efficiency of production. The concept of non-dissolution
only in cases in which a breakdown into smaller units would cause a "drastio
reduction" in operating efficiency, as stated in FEC-230, does not appear
in the Japanese law, although the law gives the HCLC the general power to
make such decisions.
Article 4. Policy with Respect to Excessive Concontrations Which Are
To Be Dissolved
Point a. This provision has been implemented in Imperial Ordinance
233, which established the Holding Company Liquidation (HCLC). Plans for
the liquidation of the principal holding companies have been approved, and
implementstion has been partially effected.
Point b. Imperial Ordinance 567 of 1946, concerning the restriotion
of `security holdings, etc., by companies designated by Imperial Crdinance
657 of 1945, provides (Article 4) that companies so designated shall draw
up plans for the disposal of stocks they hold in other concerns and submit
them to the HCLC for approval. Artiole 10 of the Law Relating to Prohibi-
tion and Private Monopolyand Methods of Preserving Fair Trade (Anti-Trust
Law) of April 1947, states that non-financial companies shall not acquire
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
stocks with voting rights of another company.
Point c. Articies 11 and 12 of Imperial Ordinance 567 of 1946,
provide that officers and directors of designated operating companies shall
give up all but one such position. By mid-Soptember 1947, a total of 1,8s0
executives had been removed because of their interlocking directorate posi-
tions.
Point d. Specific provision is made for the termination of contractual
and service arrangements of designated companies in Articles 14 and 15 of
Imperial Ordinance 567 of 1946. The prohibition against resumption of similar
arrangements is not specifically provided for in Japanese legislation, although
HCLC powers are sufficiently broad to accomplish such action.
Point e. Patent policies and procedures are under consideration in
SCAP, but as far as is known, no changes that would implemont the provisions
of this paragraph have been made in the prewar Japanese Patent Law.
Point f.
The Anti-Trust Law provides in Chapter IV, Artiole 15,
thet there shall be no mergers without the permission of the Fair Trade Commission.
Article 5. Treatment of Personnel in Excessive Concentrationsl
The provisions of FEC-230 concerning treatment of "all individuals who
I The revision of FEC-230 now being considered by the US states: "The number of
prsons subjected to the above provisions should be relatively small, and
the provisions should not be administered in such manner as to deprive Japanese
industry of persons with essential managerial or technical skills."
The recently enacted Law for the Termination of the Zaibatsu Family Control
is in line with this provision. It states that, if replacements are unobtain-
able, officials may be retained in their positions for one year in cases where
the companies concerned or their successors "are essential to the reconstruo-
tion of the national economy" and the services of these officials "are indis-
pensable for management" of the companies.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
have exercised controlling power in or over any excessive concentratiom-
covered by the
of economio power" are/amended HCLC Ordinance (Imperial Ordinance 591 of
December 1946). The HCLC has designated the'fifty-six persons who were
the heads of families that compose the ten major Zaibatsu clans, has taken
over their securities, and has established control over their properties.
These persons are prohibited from holding or assuming positions as officials
in any company for a period of ten years.
The Japanese Government has also taken action against "all other
persons likely to act on behalf of the individuals described above" in the
Law for Termination of the Zaibatsu Family Control, passed in December 1947.
This action, however, is not as severe as that called for in Artiole 5 of
FEC-230. The Japanese law is designed to terminate Zalbatsu control or in-
fluence over Zalbatsu enterprises exercised by (a) members of the immediato
families of the fifty-six previously designated Zaibatsu family heads and
(b) ofiicials who have been appointed by and who have represented Zaibatsu
interests and may therefore be expected to continue to wield much influence.
The purpose of this new law is purported to be to avoid defeat of the
deconcentration program through the use of "fronts" related to the Zaibatsu
by ties of blood or by feudalistic allegiance. The law provides, however,
that Zaibatsu appointees shall be restricted from holding office for ton
years only in any restricted concern identified with that Zaibatsu family
by which such persons were appointed. They may serve as officers in all
other companies either on or off the restricted list. They are not divested
of any of their securities and they are not excluded from any public office.
The members of the immediate families of the fifty-six previously designated
Zaibatsu, however, cannot hold office for ten years in any restricted concern.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
They may hold office in any company not on the restricted list and are not
excluded from holding public office.
Article 6. Compensation of Divested Holdings
Indemnification of individuals divested of their holdings is provided
for in Imperial Ordinance 233, Article 15, which states that compensation
shall be paid in non-negotiable government bonds of ton years' duration.
Since the amount of this compensation has not been determined, as yet, it is
too early to determine the impact of the provision in the future holdings
of the Zaibatsu.
According to Articles 1l and 13 of Imperial Ordinance 233, the HCLC
is to issue receipts when receiving transfers of properties end is to
determine "with the approval of the Prime Minister the amounts to be paid in
redemption of the receipts that have been issued for the transferred properties.
Such amounts shall be based upon but shall not exceed the net proceeds"
derived from the final disposition of these properties.
It is still too early to determine whether or not the Zaibatsu
will be able to buy back a place of power. They have been affected by the
capital levy and other taxes, but until their compensation is determined and
until suoh factors as the prevailing price level ten years from now and the
operation of the Anti-Trust Law can be estimated, their eventual status in
the economy must remain a matter for speculation.
Point a. According to the Adjustment and Coordination of Disposal.
of Securities Law of January 17, 1947, which established the Securities Coordinat-
ing Liquidating Commission (SCLC), decisions as to selling prioce, time of sale,
etc., will be aimed at aohieving the widest possible distribution of sales.
However, there appears to be some conflict between this law's stated intention
of securing as wide a distribution of securities as possible and the apparent
desire of the Japanese HCLC to sell the shares at something approaching their
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
real value. Intent to sell at a figure approximating the real value was
ovidenced at the eleventh meeting of the HCLC on June 19, 1947, when it
was decided that the following factors, among others, would be taken into
consideration in determining the disposition price of transferred securitiess
The current market value of the securities concerned
(when there is no market value, the market value of
the securities of other companies belonging to a
similar category of industry).
b.
The financial position of the stock-issuing company
concerned.
C.
The income-earning capacity of the stock-issuing
company concerned.
d.
The future prospects of the business of the stock-
issuing company concerned.
Point b.
The 90 percent or more tax called for in FEC-230 has not
been imposed by Japanese legislation according to available information. No
steeply progressive tax on funds to be assigned to the Zaibatsu has been
specifically imposed or provided for, although taxes such as the capital levy
have had some effect in this direction.
Point c. As far as is known, no Japanese tax has been imposed on
gains realized through resale of divested holdings.
Point d. Imperial Ordinance 233 contains provisions similar to those
called for in this point.
Point e. No such provision has been made in Japanese Legislation
to date. This stage of the program has not been reached.
Point f. No such provision has been made in Japanese legislation to
date.
Article 7. Liquidation of Divested Holdings
The provisions of this article suggest administrative procedures
that need not be and have not been inoorporated into Japanese law.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
Points a and b. There are no known written provisions in Japa
law covering these points.
Point o. The SCLC has been impowered to conduct negotiated sales
of securities and properties.
Point d. This procedure falls within the general powers of the
SCLC.
Article 8. Sale of Divested Holdings
In the sales of divested securities and properties, the HCLC has
announced that its policy will be to effect the widest distribution of
income and ownership of the means of production and trade.
Point a. The problem of nationalization and/or state control has
been vigorously debated in Japan.
Point b. The stated policy of the HCLC is in agreement ith that
stated in FEC-230, with the possible exception of the policy relating to
sales of securities to trade unions. At the eleventh meeting of the HCLC
(June 19, 1947) the matter of sales to unions was considered. The chairman
of the HCLC took the position that employees as individuals should be given
preference over employees' unions. Although final decision on preventing
unions from becoming preferential purchasers (either directly or through
acquiring voting rights of stocks owned by members) was postponed, it appears
that the policy to date in Japan is to prevent unions as groups from acquir-
ing shares or voting rights of shares. At the eleventh meeting of the HCLC
it was decided that:
"In the disposition of transferred securities the following
procedure shall be followed:
(a) "The shares issued by Restricted Concerns and their
Subsidiaries and Affiliated Companies shall be sold
by this Commission preferably to the employees of
the stock-issuing concerns, and secondarily to
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
residents of the localities in which their
factories are located.
(b) In the case of the shares of those companies,
other than the companies mentioned in the
preceding item (a), disposition may be made
by public salo, but the principle of giving
preference to the employees of the stock-
issuing concerns will be kept in mind.
(o) Every effort shall be made to prevent the undue
concentration of economic power through the
purchase of these securities."
Point c. This policy is followed by the HCLC, the SCLC. and other
Japanese groups concerned with the disposal of securities. The Japanese
Anti-Trust Law(Articles 10, 11, 12, and 14) also limits security holdings
with the intent of preventing excessive concentration through owmership
of securities.
Point d. This is HCLC policy. Reports are insufficient to indicate
how well this policy is working.
Point e. The reported policy of the HCLC and the SCLC has been to
screen sales, but there have been some reports indicating that the success of
the screening plan to date is questionable.
Article 9. Liquidation of Zaibatsu Financial Enterprises
No decision appears to have been made as yet on liquidation of
Zaibatsu financial enterprises through methods suggested in this Article
of FEC-230.
Article l0. Sources of Credit
The Japanese appear to be extremely hesitant to consider changes
in the Japanese financial structure. The government is at present acting
as a major creditor through the Reconstruction Finance Bank. There has been
no significant change in the number of independent sources of credit, although
the Excessive Concentration Law and the Law for Readjustment and Reorganization
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
of'Financial Institutions provide a legal basis for suoh ohanges.
Point a. The Japanese have taken no action to split up merged
financial institutions.
Point b. The Excessive Concentration Law can be, but has not as
yet been, applied to financial institutions.
Article Il. Financial Alliances
Point a. As far as is known, the Japanese have taken no action on
this point.
Point b. This point is implemented by Article ll of the Anti-Trust
Law.
Point c. The Anti-Trust Law (Article 14) does not specifically mention
financial companies, but it does limit holdings of officers and employees of
companies to the extent suggested in FEC-230.
Point d. As far as is known, this point has not been implemented.
Article 12. Elimination of Financial Discrimination
Point a. Iio long-term system of deposit insurance has been instituted.
Point b. No fixed system has been instituted for redeposit of postal
savings funds.
Point c. There has been fundamental revision in banking procedure or
inspection, with the exception of provisions for SCAP supervision of loans
to restricted concerns and for the submission of periodic bank reports to SCAP.
The Financial Institutions Reorganization and Readjustment Law provides, on a
temporary basis, for bank reporting and for examination of banks during the
readjustment period.
Point d. There has been no fundamental change in procedures applicablo
to special banks. These banks are created by special legislation such as the
Reconversion Finance Bank Law.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
10
Point •.
The recemttamendment of the Bank of Japan Law has made
no fundamental change affecting owmership and directorship.
Point f. The designated bank system and financial control associa-
tions have been officially abolished. Competition for customers is limited
by the restricted availability of bank funds and general economio control
legislation of a temporary nature. The Fair Trade Commission has instituted
a suit against banks, charging that their so-called "interest rate agreemont"
is a violation of the new Anti-Trust law. It has therefore been suggested
by the Bank of Japan that a money interest control law be passed as a
means of stabilizing interest rates.
Point g. Although there is no available information referring
specifically to employees in the Ministry of Finance and government banks,
the National Public Service Law of 1947 prohibits government employees from
conducting or holding office in any profit-making enterprise and from
accepting office in any profit-making enterprise conneoted with the duties
of his public position for two years following his retirement. A civil
servant who has holdings in a private enterprise sufficiently great to enable
him to participate in its management may be required by the Civil Service
Commission to report those holdings and to divest himself of his holdings
in whole or in part.
Article 13. Government Support of Industrial Monopolies
Point a.
Control associations have been ordered abolished. Wherover
necessary and feasible kodans, or public corporations, are being established
to replace the old control associations. Capital for these kodans is fully
subscribed by the government. Key kodans were established by separate Laws
in April 1947; four additional kodans were established in December l947.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
The principal kodan laws are:
The Shipping Publio Corporation Law
The Petroloum Distribution Publio Corporation Law
The Solid Fuel Distribution Public Corporation Law
The Industrial Reconstruction Publio Corporation Law
The Foreign Trade Publio Corporation Law
The Fertilizer Distribution Public Corporation Ordinance
The Liquor Distribution Public Corporation Law
The Feed Distribution Public Corporation Law
The Grocery Distribution Publio Corporation Law
The Oilstuffs Distribution Public Corporation Law
By September 1947 over 1,500 national control associations and control
companies had been identified and almost 700 of them either had been closed
or were in the process of being closed. About 350 control associations and
companies were designated by the Economic Stabilization Board to continue
temporarily until arrangements could be made for taking over necesss:y
functions. The process of identifying control associations and providing
for their liquidation is continuing, and plans are reportedly in preparation
for taking similar action against prefectural level associations.
Point b. It appears that wartime laws requiring governmental approval
for entry of new business or expansion of existent business have been abrogated.
The government does in, effect, exert some control over entry of new business
or business expansion through the allocation controls, etc., established by
law, but not through specific legislation. Most restrictions are purportedly
on a non-discriminatory basis, but actual operation of control may result in
some discrimination against new entry, especially since the critical state
of the economy has forced government into business to a great extent.
Point c. In general, laws currently in force give the legislative
branch of government control, but in practice the ministries retain a great
deal of control, al though they are responsible to the legislature.
Article l4. Anti-Trust Law
The Anti-Trust Law conforms to the requirements of this article.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
Artiole 15. Patent Law
No changes have been made in the Japanese Patent Law for the purpose
of implementing this article.
Article 16. Corporate Law
No known changes have been made in the Japanese Commercial Code.
Point a. The Security and Exchange Law passed by the Japanese
Diet provides for only partial implementation of this point.
Point b. No action has been taken on this point.
Point o.
This provision has been only partially implemented by
the Securities and Exchange Law.
Point d. This provision has been partially implemented by Articles
13 to 16 of the Japanese Anti-Trust Law.
Points e - g. No action has been taken on these points.
Point h. Japanese corporation law has not been amended in accordance
with this point, but Article 10 of the Anti-Trust law specifically forbids
any company, other than financial, to acquire stocks with voting rights of
another company. Acquisition of l00 percent of stocks of another company is
permitted if approved by the Fair Trade Commission.
Point i. This point does not require implementation.
Article 17. Tax and Inheritance Laws
Point a. Changes made in the Japanese Income Tax Law doe not appear
to carry out the provision that income taxes "should be very much more steeply
graduated than they are at present;" the intention of these changes is to
revise methods of collection to prevent evasion and other malpraotices that
have enabled high-inoome groups to escape the impact of income taxes.
Inheritance tax laws have been more steeply graduated than before, going up
to 60-65 percent on estates and gifts of 5 million yen or more. (The average
applicable rate on any one estate or gift of 5 million yen or more, however,
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
would be less than 60 to 65 peroent.)
Point b. This provision has been implemented to the extent that
the pertinent sections of the new Japanese Civil Code eliminate the concept
of "head of the house."
Point c. This point has been legally implemented by revisions in the
new Japanese Civil Code, but is too early to ascertain whether or not this
change will result in an actual diffusion of inherited wealth.
Point d. The concept of the house has been changed to that of the
family in the new Japanese Civil Code.
Point e. The Japanese Finance Law of 1947 provides for reduotion
in the discretionary power of the Minister of Finance in tax matters. The
Minister of Finance continues to hold important power, however, although,
as a member of the cabinet, he is responsible to the Diet. Tax rates are
fixed by the Diet.
Article 18. Polioy Concerning Preferred Purchasers
Point a. The Agricultural Cooperative Associations Law passed in
l947 establishes the principles set forth in this soction of FEC-230.
Point b. As previously stated in connection with Article 8, point
b, the HCLC apparently does not intend to regard trade unions as preferred
purchasers as specified in FEC-230.
Point c. There has been considerable discussion in the Japanose
Government about the necessity of aiding small entrepreneurs, but it appears
that there has been no actual implementation of this point as yet.
Article 19. Public Support
The HCLC and SCAP have begun to carry out a program of educating
the Japanese publio with regard to the Zaibatsu program. Various means
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
have been used, inoluding newsreels, public opinion polls, weekly press
interviews, radio breadoasts, round-table conferences, and study groups.
as well as magasine and newspaper articles. It is still too early to
determine the effeotiveness of this program or whether it will fully imple-
ment this article.
Article 20. Japanese Government
It is difficult to determine the extent to which attempts have been
made "to deprive the Japanese Government of its former pro-Zaibatsu oharacter."
Little has been done to oust old line bureaucrats who have been pro-Zaibatsu,
but reports indicate that efforts have been made to prevent persons with
pro-Zaibatsu connections from assuming key government positions.
Point a. SCAP policy has been to screen carefully those individuals
chosen to staff the new public agencies established to carry out the nti-
Zaibatsu program.
Point b. Legislative supremacy over the bureaucracy has been clearly
established in the new Japanese Constitution, which went into effoct on May
3, l947. Recent economic legislation, however, does give the administration
a wide range of discretionary authority, although this is also true of
similar legislation in the US, the inexperience of Japanese Diet members
in exercise of legislative control over administrative agencies has in
practice permitted the bureaucracy to enjoy a considerable degree of
independence. At this time the most effective cheok on bureaucratio
usurpation of legislative functions is the lose SCAp surveillance over
administrative agencies.
●
Point c. Reports are insufficient to provide a basis for determination
of the extent, if any, to whichthis section of FEC-230 has been implemented.
Point d. Article l03 of the National Public Service Law, passed in
l947 in the first session of the National Diet, prohibits any civil servant
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
from conducting or holding office in any profit-making onterprise and
from accepting office in any profit-making enterprise conneoted with the
duties of his public position for two years following his retirement from
government service. A civil servant who has holdings in a profit-making
enterprise sufficiently great to enable him to participate in its managoment
while employed in government service may be required by the Civil Service
Commission to report these holdings. On the basis of such reports, the
Commission may require the individual involved to divest himself of his
holdings in whole or in part.
Point e. It has been traditional for the Japanese Governmont to
publish twice a year a civil service registry, the Shokuin Roku, containing
information regarding officials in the government It is not known whether
this practice has been continued during the occupation, but the government
has been required to furnish SCAP'with monthly lists of the employees in the
various ministries.
Point f. This principle was established both in Article 17 of
the new Japanese Constitution and in the State Redress Law of September
17,1947.
Article 21. United Nations and Neutral Interests
Reports and SCAP directives indicate that SCAP has taken measures
designed to protect the interests of nationals of members of the United Nations
in Japan during this transition period and that principles and procedures are
being formulated to protect the interests of United Nations' nationals who
were investors in Japan as of December 1, 194l.

FEC-230
In Lay, 1947, the United States Government submitted to the Far
Eastern Jonaission a policy proposal dealingwith"Excessive Conoentra-
tions of conomic Powers in Jap n." The Secretariat of the Far
Eastern Com issio. assigned to this proposal a designation, FEc 230,
classificd it "confidential", placed it upon the agenda of the Economio
Col ittee which, in tur, referred it to one of its sub-committees.
Thus, the paper FEC 230 becane a part of the business of the Far Eastern
Conmission a.d ceased to be t.e property of the United States. The
United States has renained at liberty, of course, to modify its attitude
towards its own ori inal proposal. If the United States should submit
amendnents for the consideration of the TEC, the Secretariat of the FEC
woul d, normally, assign to such amendments a new designation which would
then become the business of the Comission. Technically such amend-
ments could include complete disapproval of the substance of FEC-230
However, the paper itself could not be withdrawn by the United States
without a Far Eastern Comnission dec sion.
Dr. Blakeslee agrees with me in the above opinion.

TO : A-T, A-S
DATE: February 3, 1948
FROM : OE - Roswell H. Whitman
suject: Proposed revisions of "Policy on Excessive Concentrations of Economie
Power in Japan", as revised.
To meet some of the questions raised in the attached letter of
January 16, 1948 from Assistant Secretary of the Army Gray, the
following changes are recommended in the attached revision of FEC-230.
1. Add to paragraph 2:
Standards for the designation of an "important segment of
business" and of the enterprises which are excessive concentra-
tions within each such important segment should be developed.
2. Amend first sentence of paragraph 3 to read:
686
Dissolution of excessive concentration should not be
carried out:
(a) to a degree which, because of technological considera-
9
tions (e.g., the characteristics of their physical
02
properties) would cause a material reduction in operating
efficiency; or
/2-348
(b) in the case of public utilities which do not lend them-
selves to competitive operation, to a degree which would
cause a material reduction in service to the public.
3. Amend paragraph 8(a) by deleting "overriding" and replacing
it with "fundamental".
4. Amend paragraph ll(c) by deleting "all vestiges of private
ownership" and substitute "private participation in the ownership."
5. Add to paragraph 17:
"A unit should be set up in the appropriate Ministry,
specifically devoted to supplying small business with aid in
the procurement of credit, of technological information, of
materials, and of labor."
6. Amend paragraph 18 by deleting period and adding:
"...through
4880

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
"...through such means as provision for access to recent
literature in other countries about problems of industrial
organization, widespread publication of the HCLC's and ITC's
findings regarding excessive concentrations, and provision for
contact between the HCLC and FTC and similar bodies in other
countries."
The changes proposed above do not meet Mr. Gray's views in
every instance, but they do treat with all of the points raised
in Mr. Gray's letter of January 16, with the exception of those
listed below:
1. Mr.Gray's letter asks whether the wording in paragraph 4(b)
of the proposed policy statement is needlessly strict when it
says that the units into which excessive concentrations are broken
down should be divested of any securities which they may hold in
any other concerns, including concerns not a part of any excessive
concentration. This provision has been the subject of careful
reconsideration from time to time in the past; in each case, it
has been concluded that the retention of this provision is extremely
desirable. Need for the provision stems from the fact that numerous
cases have been noted by SCAP in the past in which restricted
concerns have shifted their customers, inventory, and working force
to seemingly unrelated enterprises in which the restricted concerns
had only a small investment. Since the size of an investment is,
at best, an uncertain guide to the measure of influence which one
Japanese firm exercises upon another, it is deemed highly desirable
to wipe the slate clean as regards intercorporate investments and
to permit restricted concerns to start afresh on the same footing
as other concerns in this regard. This will mean that re-acquisi-
tion of the securities of other corporations will be subject only to
the provisions of the Fair Trade Practices Act.
2. Mr. Gray's letter suggests that paragraph 4(c) of the
proposed policy statement seems to forbid certain kinds of "arrange-
ments which have been found to be necessary in this country; i.e.,
vhere monopolies have been dissolved under the Sherman Act, the
courts have required the monopolist to assist the development of
competing units by giving technical aid and by supplying experienced
personnel." V.e have no knowledge of an "arrangement"such as
described by Mr. Gray being ordered by the courts in connection with
the operation of the Sherman Act. What Mr. Gray may have in mind
is the fact that, under certain decrees, firms in violation of the
Sherman

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Sherman Act have sometimes been required to license certain blocks
of patents non-exclusively. But paragraph 4(c) would not prohibit
such an arrangement.
3. Referring to paragraph 6 of the proposed policy statement,
Mr. Gray asks whether it is intended that companies (as distinguished
from individuals) will be indemnified in connection with the
divestiture of intercorporate stockholdings. However, the question
of indemnification does not arise in regard to companies since
the proposed policy statement contains no provision for the seisure
and liquidation of company assets, whereas it does provide for the
seizure and liquidation of individuals' assets. Companies yb
required by the proposed policy statement to divest themselves of
holdings in other compenies--a divestiture which they can accomplish
by distributing such securities to their own security-holders,
by liquidation of the securities in the open market, or by the
exchange of such securities for some property or other assets which
they desire. In none of these cases does the problem of indemni-
fication arise.
4. Referring to paragraph l4 of the proposed policy statement,
Mr. Gray suggests the desirability of adding a sentence which would
amplify the treatment to be accorded patents in deconcentration
proceedings. The proposed amplification, however, is more confusing
than helpful. In any case, since the subject matter of Japanese
patents is being treated in a separate paper (SANACC 369) it is
considered undesirable to introduce matter on patents not necessary
to the purpose of this paper at this point.

1. Appendix "C".
2.
Mr. Gray's comments on
proposed "Policy on Ex-
cessive Concentrations of
Economic Power in Japan".

The following comments and suggestions relate to the indicated paragraphs is
the draft discussed in the office of the Assistant Secretary of the Aray,
Saturday, 10 January 1948:
2. In view of the vagueness of the words "restricts", "impairs" and
"opportunity" it would probably be helpful if we could state that only
in extraordinary circumstances would units representing less than a
specified percentage of the market be dissolved. The percentage to be
specified should be recommended by the people who know the Japanese
situation. Five per cent is too small.
Does "any important .segment of business" refer to the competitive
situation in Japan as a whole or is this intended to assure competition
in each geographical area? The former seems to be more practical.
3. To reflect the consideration of public utilities in Sec. 8(b); i.e.,
"enterprises such as public utilities which do not lend themselves to
competitive operation," suggest adding "or service to the public" after
the words "operating efficiency" in the first sentence of this section.
Suggest: "All excessive concentrations of economic power (both finan-
cial and non-financial) not referred to in paragraph 3 should be
effectively separated, to the extent the characteristics of their
physical properties permit, into individual enterprises which will be
independent of each other..."
Is the wording in 4b. needlessly strict when it says: "be divested of
any securities which they may hold in other concerns..."?
Subparagraph 4c. seems to forbid the very kind of arrangements which
have been found to be necessary in this country; i.e., where monopolies
have been dissolved under the Sherman Act, the courts have required the
monopolist to assist the development of competing units by giving tech-
nical aid and by supplying experienced personnel.
6. Is it intended thst companies will not be indemnified? In splitting up
a large unit, care should be taken that the unit is not so divested of
its properties that it cannot continue as an effective competitor of the
new units created.
8. The deletion of the word "overriding" in line 2, paragraph 8(a) would
not change the meaning but would remove a needless implication of ruth-
lessness.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.97)
CONFIDENTIAL
ll. The words "to encourage competition among credit institutiona" seem
superfluous. Paragraph ll(c) first sentence would be less oratorical
and mean the same thing if the words "all vestiges of" were eliminated.
14.
To protect the incentive to invt, Fould it not be wise to add 
second sentence: "Patents for a limited period of years should be
permitted, however, as long as the patent holder does not thereby
achieve an economic power disproportionate to that of competitors in
the field."
17. This section appears to be extremely general. If it refers to specific
conditions in Japan for wich a remedy is needed, it should be made
more specific or the section should be eliminated.
18. Thi: section adds nothing to the document as a statement of policy, tells
CAP something which CAp is in a position to know even better than poople

is likely to be misunderstood by vongress anu the aerlcan people. The
