=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.97)
DATB: September 30, 1948
TO
E. - Mr. Paul H. Nitze
DClR
Present status of the Zaibatsu program in Japan.
File
SUBJECT:
I have described below in very brief summary the
present status of the Zaibatsu program.
1.
Legislative Program
A considerable number of laws, Imperial ordinances
cabinet orders, and other legislative and quasi-legislative
enactments relating to the Zaibatsu program are on the
Japanese books. As the program has evolved, permanent
legislation has replaced interim orders and at some points
overlaps appear to have developed. On the whole, however,
the legislative program now appears to be fairly well
integrated and to consist of the following main features:
Files
Central
This Document Must Be Returned
a. A Holding Company Liquidation Commission
Ordinance creates a Commission which is charged with the
temporary task of directing the reorganization of exist-
ing "excessive concentrations". More specifically, the
Commission is empowered to designate "Zaibatsu" individual
and holding companies, the assets of which are to be
sequestered and sold to the public; the plan provides that
the proceeds of such liquidation are to be turned back to
the individuals involved in the form of non-negotiable
Government bonds, after deduction of taxes and other
liabilities. The necessary designations under this
ordinance have already been completed and involve fifty-
six persons, comprising the heads of ten major Zaibatsu
families, and eighty-three holding companies. The
designated holding companies are primarily family com-
panies organized for the purpose of holding securities of
other companies and controlled by the fifty-six designated
persons.
b. A "Law for the Elimination of Excessive Concen-
trations of Economic Power" authorizes the Holding Company
Liquidation Commission to designate enterprises, whether
of the holding company or operating type, which constitute
"excessive concentrations", and to direct their reorganiza-
4.602
tion. In accordance with the terms of its law, the
Commission last February promulgated a series of "standards"
DO/R
for determining the companies which would be subject t
S
examination as "excessive concentrations". Pursuant to
Anal_Hw
tse
S/H
-3048
2
Cat.
3121

=== Page 220 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
these standards, some 325 non-financial companies were
designated by the Commission for investigation, of which
194 were later removed from the designated list. Ac-
cordingly, the Holding Company Liquidation Commission is
now concerned with the reorganization of not more than
131 companies, and it is understood that only a minority
of these companies at best will actually have to be re-
organized.
c. An antitrust law creates a permanent Fair Trade
Commission, prohibits unreasonable restraints of domestic
and international trade, and lays down certain limitations
on interlocking directorates, intercorporate stockholdings,
mergers, consolidations, and the like; unlike the other
enactments, this is long-run legislation intended primarily
to prevent the recurrence of the zaibatsu type of structure
in the future. Numerous orders have been issued under the
law which are intended to implement one or another of the
law's many provisions. Most important among these orders
have been those leading to the dissolution of control
associations and interlocking directorates, the liquidation
of certain intercorporate stockholdings, and the liquida-
tion of certain large individual holdings in competing
companies.
d. A revision of the Commercial Code now requires
Japanese corporations to make considerably more information
available to their stockholders and the public than here-
tofore has been the case, and prevents certain obvious
devices, such as the issuance of classes of unpaid or
partially paid shares, from being used as a means of con-
trolling a company without pro rata equity ownership.
e. The Fair Trade Commission is analyzing Japanese
laws one by one, to secure the elimination of provisions
which confer special privilege or tend to eliminate com-
petition. In the month of April, 1948, for example, the
Commission advised the Government that various provisions
of the following laws were inconsistent with the Antitrust
law: the Salt Monopoly Act, the Savings Bank Act, the
Insurance Act Enforcement Regulations, the Dairy Farming
Adjustment Act, the Law Regulating the Export of Marine
Products, the Forest and Industry Association Law, and
others.
While some of the provisions of the various enactments
described above will probably need amending in the light
of
3171

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
of experience, they represent a fairly complete legis-
lative program.
2. Execution of the Program
Various aspects of the program are in very different
states of completion:
a. Contracts containing provisions which are in
restraint of trade have been declared void. Therefore,
except insofar as continuous enforcement will be necessary,
this stage of the program is completed. Indications are
that there is a substantial disposition on the part of
the Fair Trade Commission vigorously to enforce this aspect
of the program. Almost every month, important antitrust
cases are completed against various persons and groups who
have continued to do business on the old stand. In
February, l948, for example, five trade associations and
three individual companies submitted consent decrees with
respect to charges of violating the Antitrust Act. In the
same month the Commission undertook an investigation of
restraints of trade in the book distribution field and
motion picture field. In the month of May, three motion
picture companies were found guilty of having violated the
Antitrust Act. Each month important "control associations",
heretofore permitted to perform functions in rationing and
price control, have been ordered dissolved.
b. It is almost impossible to appraise the extent
to which plans have progressed for the dissolution of non-
essential intercorporate ties. The top family holding
companies have been ordered to dissolve and, according to
our information, havefor the most part either submitted
plans for their dissolution or actually completed their
dissolution. In addition, a very large number of other
companies have submitted plans to the Fair Trade Commission
for the divestiture of intercorporate stockholdings which
fall outside the pale of that law, but it is unclear to
what extent these plans have been put into effect. The
one area in which there clearly has been little action is
in the dissolution of the complex financial and personal
ties among the tier of secondary holding and holding-
operating companies which typified each of the Zaibatsu
structures. Some of these ties have presumably been
affected by the general limitations placed on all inter-
locking directorates and intercorporate share holdings.
However,

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
However, the Holding Company Liquidation Commission's
approach, which consists of appraising the relationship
of specific groups of companies in terms of all of the
varied intercorporate ties commonly employed in Japan--
e.g., central accounting offices, common engineering
services, cross-licensing agreements, and the like--has
not yet resulted in a significant number of reorganizations.
And, finally, with one or two outstanding exceptions, no
progress whatever has been made in dealing with oversized
and technologically incongruous single companies. Outfits
like Mitsubishi Heavy Industry--which under a single
corporate charter operates everything from drug stores to
steel mills and whose loans and advances from the second
largest bank in Janan amount to ten times the capital and
reserves of that bank--have not been touched. Whether
in fact such companies will be considered at all by the
Holding Company Liquidation Commission depends upon the
way in which its standards are interpreted. The standards
of the Commission, if revised in accordance with recent
suggestions of the Deconcentration Review Board, will
provide that no order should be issued under the decon-
centration law unless there is a "showing of a prima facie
case" thet the company restricts competition. It is
unclear whether such a standard will be construed so
restrictively as to prevent many necessary reorganizations.
c. Under the various programs outlined above, a
number of different groups will have occasion to dispose
of large amounts of securities: the Holding Company Liquida-
tion Comnission, which has sequestered the securities of
Zaibatsu individuals and their family holding companies;
various companies which have been or will be ordered to
dispose of certain of their portfolio securities; and
individual stockholders who, because of sizeable holdings
in potentially competing companies, may be subject to a
disposal order by the Fair Trade Commission. All exist-
ing data on the extent of the necessary liquidation are
fragmentary. Information regarding the disposal operations
of the Holding Company Liquidation Commission, however,
are more complete than for the other groups. As regards
the Commission's liquidation program, two facts are clear:
(1) not more than 20% of the securities to be sold by
the Commission have yet been sold, and (2) the Commission
has deliberately refrained from liquidating in large amounts,
partly because it prefers not to liquidate the securities
of companies which may be subject to considerable future
reorganization

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
reorganization and partly because it is concerned with
minimizing the effects of its sales on market prices.
In almost every recent offering made by the Commission,
however, the securities offered have been taken up and
frequently oversubscribed.
3.
Outstanding Issues
The following problems are the chief issues with which
we should be concerned in any future action affecting the
Zaibatsu program:
a. The program in Japan has a certain amount of
indigenous support. If it is to be successful, that
support must be retained and extended. Our future actions
must be gauged in the light of their effect upon such
indigenous support.
b. From the first, major emphasis has been placed
upon the importance of increasing the number of independent
sources of credit in Japan if any measure of competitive enter-
prise is to be achieved. The original directive of the.
Joint Chiefs of Staff stated "...It shall be the policy of
the Supreme Commander...to favor a program for the dis-
solution of the large industrial and banking combinations
of a great part of Japan's trade and industry". Except
insofar as regulations of general application may have
affected the banks, nothing has been done with respect to
the existing enormous concentrations of assets in a few
Japanese banks. This has been due partly to the fact that
the problem of deconcentration as it relates to banks has
rested in the Finance Division of SCAP rather than in the
Cartels and Antitrust Division. The former Division has
been primarily preoccupied with the technical aspects of
bank operations and, understandably enough, has been
inimical to any program which might disturb the status quo.
Nevertheless, the banking system of Japan will perforce
exercise a major voice in determining the nature and mag-
nitude of the expansion of Japanese industrial and commercial
life; this is particularly true for that country because
the Japanese have almost no investment banking machinery
other than that controlled by the commercial banks. The
remainder of the program may well prove futile if the
problem of banking concentration is not properly handled.
c. A very large volume of securities still remain
to be liquidated. The problem is to select the proper line
between

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
between a cautious and necessarily prolonged program of
liquidation, on the one hand, and an incautious but briefer
period of liquidation on the other. Questions to be
answered in this connection are: Will the existence of a
supply of stock overhanging the market for a long term
have a more depressing effect upon Japanese capital markets,
and through them upon Japanese recovery, than a short
term but more pronounced depression in Japanese stock
prices? In that connection, what is the absorptive
capacity of the Japanese public for the offered securities?
Will prolonged uncertainty as to the identity of new owners
of Japanese industry have a greater or lesser effect upon
managerial enterprise than a swifter but less selective
determination of such new owners? Will the prolonged
possession of securities in the hands of a Government
agency stimulate demands for the socialization of enter-
prise? Two years ago, on balancing these considerations,
the Dep.rtment's answer was to urge strongly the rapid
liquidation of the securities involved. The answer may
conceivably be different today.

represents a more vigorous attempt than its predecessor to bring

legislation as internationally recognized." It would thus satisfy
the criticism commonly voiced by American businessmen that monop-

The heart of the proposal lies in its revision of Article 10.
The unamended version (see Enclosure 1, in which pertinent extracts
of the law are quoted from the Official Gazette for ready reference)
forbids intercorporate holding of securities, with very narrow ex-
ceptions. The Conmission's plan of September 16, though forbidding
intercorporate holdings in restraint of trade, would have allowed
companies capitalized at more than 50,000,000 yen to hold stock in
other companies after obtainin the approval of the Fair Trade Com-
mission; companies capitalized at less than that figure would have
been required only to report stock acquisitions. The September 25
proposal, on the other hand, would in all cases require only subse-
quent registration. The Anti-Trust and Cartels Division, consider-
ing both proposals unacceptable, has drafted an amended Article 10
allowing unrestricted intercorporate holdings, provided that the
two companies are not in competition, and that the purchasing com-
pany does not already oin stock in a company competing with the
company whose stock it proposes to acquire.
Article 6, requiring advance approval of international con-
tracts or agreements continuing "for a considerable period of time,
vould be deleted under the most recent proposal. The earlier pro-
posal would have deleted the final paragraph only; the third para-
graph would have been revised to require registration with the
Commission within ten days after concluding an international agree-
ment.
Article 1l, regardin financial institutions, would have been
revised under the old proposal to allow institutions with a capital
of no more than 50,000,000 yen to acquire unrestricted holdings in
other companies; the present law allows such holdings only by com-
panies with a capital of less than 5,000,000 yen. The 1ater pro-
posal, on the other hand, would require only registration of stock
acquisitions by financial institutions. The Anti-Trust and Cartels
Division is of the opinion that revision of Article 1l is not per-
tinent at this time, and should await a general reform of banking
legislation.
Article 12, under the original proposal, would have been
amended to require registration vith the Commission of transactions
in which a company acquires debentures of another company in ex-
cess of an amount equivalent to 25 per cent of the capital of the
second company. Such transactions are now forbidden. The more re-
cent proposal would delete Article 12 entirely, thus allowing un-
limited holdin of debentures.

Private Interests Lobby on Monopoly Lav: Revision - Nihon Keizai -
17 Sep 48. Translator: T. 1itsuhashi.
(DD)
Full Translation:
The revision of the Anti-Monopoly Law is imperative in connec-
tion vith the introduction of private foreign capital, and its
early realization is bein urged by private circles. The Govern-
ment, in response to this demand, has been speeding the drafting
of an amendment bill under the guidance of the Fair Trade Commis-
sion, and the bill is expected to be submitted to the special Diet
session if possible.
However, as yet lovernment an! private circles have not
reached an agreement on details of the bill. Consequently, it is
expected that private business circles will make a strong bid to
have their opinions reflected in the revision, and the lobbying
surrounding the revision of the Anti-Honopoly Law is attracting
keen attention.
Economic organizations, includin the Federation of Economic
Organizations, the Economic Friends Society, the Tokyo Chamber of
Commerce and Industry, and the Japan Industrial Association, are
now discussin: the revision of the Anti-lonopoly Law. Disputed
articles of the law wi:ich are directly concerned with the introduc-
tion of private forei n capital and the reopening of private for-
eign trade include (a) the problem of international agreements in
Article V1; (b) restricted possession of shares among corparations
in Article X; (c) restricted possession of debentures by corpora-
tions in Article XII; (d) restrictions on the holding of concurrent
posts in Article XIIr. These items incidentally will necessitate
the revision of Article II, XI and XV.
Opinions of financial circles on the revision of these items
are:
1. (a) Article VI, lause 2 should be revised to read "the
precedin provision vill not apply in the following cases."
(1) A reements or contracts with foreign businessmen
which are absolutely necessary for the consummation of import con-
tracts on funds, raw naterials, eqipment, technology and industrial
rights, which are essential for domestic production.
(2) Then the absence of international agreements and
contracts renders tradin activities on an international basis un-
reasonably difficult.

Mr. Clarton

FROM : W. A. Rudlin 54.R.
SUBJECT:
FEC 230
My reaction to the situation presented by the memoranda to
Mr. Saltzman from E and FE on the above subject is as follows:/
1. I believe that a statement should be made along the lines indi-
cated by Mr. Nitze. It should, however, be made in FEC, whether or not
y=
it is also made outside by Mr. Royall and Mr. Lovett.
2. I believe also a message should be sent to SCap along the lines
y
indicated by Mr. Nitse. As he indicates, it is important to show both
to the Japanese and the Deconcentration Review Board that we are con-
cerned with implementation of the deconcentration program.
3. I am convinced that "1" and "2" above will not happen. M, and
more particularly the Army, Department, will never agree to a statement
which contains a reaffirmation of the deconcentration objective. Their
whole inclination over many months past has been either by statements or
by silence to convey the strong impression that not only FEC 230 as a
b
paper, but the actual implementation of SCAP's program should be reviewed,
reconsidered, and relared. The same is true with the message to SCAP.
Any tightening of deconcentration standards and insistence on ertension
of the program (as distinct from the basic directive) to the fields of
0
banking and finance would, I am sure, be resisted strongly and indefinitely
2
by F and the Army.
4. If the above pessimism with regard to the Army is realistic,
progress along the lines reconmended by Mr. Nitze could only be made
within a reasonable time by taking this matter up on the highest levels.
448
For this to be possible, we would need a very firm position within State.
I am equally sure we will not get this, but there is only one way to rind
out, which is for Mr. Saltzman to call a meeting of Messrs. Nitze and
Butterworth for the purpose of determing (a) whether their positions are
as far apart as they appear to be from their memoranda, and (b) to
emphasize to them that each should be prepared to compromise since the
positions are so widely divergetand since the Department has been for
nearly a year, and remains vulnerable in this whole matter.
5. All this boils down to the fact that E wants as much deconcentra-
tion as it can get, and FE and Army want as little as possible. This
dirference is basic, and will have to be resolved on the Assistant Secretary
level before any further consideration is given to more or less procedural
matters such as what kind of public statement can be made, where, and by
whom. Perhaps "procedural" is the wrong word, since the reason for making

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.96)
QUNELDEIRLAL
-2
à public statement is much the same as for sending a message for the
Deconcentration Review Board. The message would tell the Board to get
on with things and do a real job. The purpose of the statement would
be not only to reaffirm the U.S. objective, but thereby to make sure
that the Japanese Government would energetically administer the legiale-
tion it already has on the books. As indicated above, therefore, both
Mr. Nitze's recommendations would amount to giving the program a strong
shot in the arm.
6. The most hopeful thing that I can see, and even this would be
a big concession from Army and , is egreement on a statement in o
which recounts in detail what has been done under the program, reaffirms
U.S. attachment to the "hasic objective", and draws the conclusion that
no paper is necessary.
7. Failing such a compromise, I believe Mr. Saltzman can only
report to Mr. Lovett that a year's close attention to this problem has
produced only a wide split within State and limy, and that no further
time should be lost in arriving at a solution on the Secretary level.

The Holding Jonpany Liqui dation Conmission of the Japanese
Government has recently announced (as reported in the enclosed arti-
cle from the Nippon Times) that the cases of 175 companies which
have been released from designation for reorganization under the
Deconcentration Lar: (Law No. 207 of 1947) but which must neverth
less submit stock-disposal plans, are to be reexamined in the
light of the four basic principles recently handed down by the
Deconcentration Review Foard and reported in our despatch No. 619.
of September 20, 1948. Specifically, the Commission is to deter-
mine whether the past activities of the companies indicate that
they have operated in restraint of trade. If no such findings.
are reached, designation under the Deconcentration Law is to be
cancelled, and the facts of the case are to be transferred to the
Fair Trade Conmission for consideration under the Anti-Monopoly
Law (Law No. 54 of 1947).
The move is theoretically designed to make the position of
the Deconcentration eview Ioard consistent and logical, it being
felt by the Foard that the 175 companies in question, all of them
admittedly lesser concentrations than the 100 still under considera
tion for reorganization, should in all justice be granted the pre
tection of the four principles to be applied to the latter. Cas
involving stock disposal, etc., are therefore to be reconsidered
on the basis of vhether or not the companies have constituted a
869
combination in restraint of trade.
As the Anti-onopoly Law and the Deconcentration Law stand
now,

ANTI-TRUST POLICY IS EASED BY HCLC
175 Concerns Slated to Le Reprobed; Lay Be Taken Off
Desiinated List
A tlree- oint policy pointing torard the easing of regulations
to be applied to 175 concerns desi nated for economic deconcentra-
tion iut not re puired to le reorganized was adopted by the Holding
Jompany Lipuidation onmission Priday.
The HeLe drafted its new olicy in light of the four basic
principles aunounccd recetl y the Deconcentration Review Board,
GHQ, SCAP.
The three points are as follows:
1. The Commission shall reinvestigate the activities
of these 175 concerns to ascertain vhether or not such facts
of monopoly as stipulated in the four basic principles existed
in their past activities on the basis of data currently in
the Jommission's hands. The conmission will not ask for fur
ther data for the proposed reinvestigations.
2. If the Commission finds no such facts of monopoly
in the inter sted concern after reinvestigation, the Commission
will cancel the current designation, and at the same time, will
report its findings to the Fair Transaction Commission.
3. If the Holding Company Li uidation Commission discovers
some facts of monopoly falling unler the four principles, the
Commission vill submit its notification stating the facts con-
cernin: the monopolistic activities of the interested concerns
to the Deconcentration Revie : oard and Japanese Government.
Fowever, even in such a case, the reorganization program for
such concern will be limited to the liquidation of its stock.
In such a case, reorganizational measures will be directed and
supervised by the HCLC, and after its completion, the HCLC
will transfer its investigation data to the Fair Transaction
Conmission.

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.99)
DRAFT STATEMENT TO THE FAR EASTERN COMMISSION RE DECONCENTRATION
Some months ago, my Government suspended its participation
in discussions in the Far Eastern Commission of a United States
policy proposal which was then under active consideration in
the Commission. This proposal, designated as FEC 230, presented
a detailed plan for the implementation of a general policy
which already had been stated in existing directives to the
Supreme Commander.
The action of the United States in suspending consideration
of its proposal, however, has led to certain questions among
the members of this Commission and among the Japanese people.
The purpose of this statement is to clarify the position of
the United States with respect to FEC 230.
Since the very first weeks of the Occupation, the Supreme
Commander has devoted a considerable part of the time and
resources of his staff to the problem of reorganizing the
financial and industrial institutions of Japan. This program
which has been based upon the Post Surrender Directive issued
December 6, 1945 and on the Far Eastern Commission's own
Basic Post-Surrender Policy for Japan, was designed to
contribute to the development of democratic and peacefully-
inclined institutions in Japan. To bring about that result,
plans were immediately developed and put into effect to
dissolve the excessive concentration of control of Japanese
finance and industry.
As a part of this program, the Supreme Commander directed
the Japanese Government to adopt various laws and to create
certain governmental bodies charged with the responsibility
of undertaking a major reorganization of the ownership and
control of Japanese industry. In the brief span of three
years

=== Page 250 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
years considerable work has been done by these bodies.
The assets of the 56 persons who comprised the heads of
the 10 major economic concentrations and the assets of the
83 holding companies controlled by these persons have been
acquired by the Government and are in process of being sold
to the Japanese public. A much larger number of companies
have been compelled to divest themselves of holdings in
and control over smaller enterprises. Such control was
exercised through intercorporate stockholdings, interlocking
directorates and similar devices. Contractual arrangements
to which these Japanese enterprises were parties which had
the effect of placing the control of production or trade in
the hands of such enterprises have been declared void. The
innumerable Control Associations through which Japanese
enterprises exercised their collective authority are being
liquidated. Action is being taken and is well advanced
toward reorganization of former savings banks, trust
companies and governmental banking institutions, making
possible the emergence of a significant number of new
commercial banks, to compete with and supplement the few
large banking combines which formerly dominated Japanese
credit sources. Finally, some scores of Japanese companies
whose present state may constitute a threat to competitive
enterprise are being scrutinized.
Substantial revisions have been effected in the basic
economic legislation of Japan. To begin with, an Antitrust
Law has been adopted and a Fair Trade Commission set up to
enforce the Law. In general, the Law seeks to restrain the
development of new combines, excessively large or powerful,
by outlawing

=== Page 251 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
agreements which restrain production or trade, by placing
limitations upon intercorporate stockholdings, inter-
locking directorates, and similar devices for the concen-
tration of corporate control, and by setting up procedures
and penalties for the enforcement of these provisions.
Other legislation now requires Japanese corporations to
make considerably more information available to their
stockholders and the public than heretofore has been the
case and generally requires the management of corporations
to adhere to much higher standards of public responsibility
in the managements of their enterprise.
Moreover, many existing laws which tended to centralize
the control of Japanese industry within a small group have.
been abrogated outright. Others have been modified
drastically. The Fair Trade Commission and other Government
agencies are analyzing still other Japanese laws. Various
laws relating to the conduct of Japanese banking have been
placed under careful scrutiny. One of the principl
objectives of the revision of Japanese banking laws is to
create a climate in which the undesirable prewar concentration
of Japanese credit in a few hands could not recur.
In all this, the Japanese Government has demonstrated
a commendable ability to comprehend Allied objectives and has
cooperatively fulfilled its obligations. The Japanese Fair
Trade Commission has prosecuted a significant series of cases
against Japanese business men who were violating one provision
or another of the statutes which seek to prevent undesirable
concentrations of Japanese industry. The Japanese Holding
Company Liquidation Commission has made a careful study of
the structure of the larger Japanese combines and, in close
cooperation

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
cooperation with the Supreme Commander, is currently
developing plans for such reorganization of these combines
as may be needed.
In view of the progress already made by SCAP and the
Japanese government, my Government no longer considers
that a further directive would be either necessary or
helpful.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Information Office
1330
9 November 1948
ACTIVITY OF 1O0O RESTRICTED CONCERNS TO BE INCREASED
SCAP announced today that the Japanese Government has been
instructed to remove the requirement for restricted concerns to
make applications regarding actions outside the normal course of
business.
This action will relieve over 1,000 Japanese companies from
having to obtain prior approval for rehabilitation projects,
actions for expansion of production facilities, and for similar
purposes now required under Imperial Ordinance 657.
This action calls for the amendment of Imperial Ordinance
657 by the deletion of Article 2 which requires restricted con-
cerns to "receive the permission of the Minister of Finance for
execution of sale, donations, and other actions causing a trans-
fer of rights of personal estate, real estate, securities and/or
other property they own, etc....."
It was emphasized that Imperial Ordinance 567 regarding di-
vesture of securities, Imperial Ordinance 233 regarding designa-
ted holding companies, and Public Law No. 207 regarding excessive
concentrations are not affected by this amendment to Imperial
Ordinance 657. However, Imperial Ordinance 567 is to be amended to
indicate clearly its application to successor companies. Likewise,
the agencies of the Japanese Government will continue such super-
vision as is necessery to prevent reformation of combines and to
carry out their responsibilities under other legislation.
With the removal of such government restrictions, it is con-
sidered that greater responsibility falls upon stockholders of
such companies to make certain that their companies are managed
efficiently and that assets are not dissipated.
In keeping with this appropriate shift of responsibility to
stockholders, SCAp has directed the Japanese Government to repeal
the Law Concerning Temporary Measures for Companies. This Law is
a wartime measure which gives broad authority to executive officers
of companies and, hence, limits considerably the authority of
stockholders. Such legislation is considered unnecessary and un-
desirable as Japan moves toward greater economic democracy.

The central problem here would seem to be whether we
can do something to stop the continuing instruotions by
SCAP anti-trust personnel to the Japanese in violation or
the principle that the Japanese shall be permitted to shake
down the reforms in their own way as long as the essemtial
elements are preserved. While the Japanese may, as Marshall
says (see below), have conflicting views as to how the
shaking-down process should proceed, the only course would
seem to be to leave them alone to work the matter out, and
to tell them that they should not "look to Headquarters for
the answers". Actually according to Mr. Hutohison (the DRB
member now in Washington) the Japanese on the Fair Trade
Commission and HCLC and others concerned could probably
do a good job of it if let alone.
Document Must Bo Returned
Unfortunately we are not supposed to have the informa-
tion in this despatoh and cannot oite it to SCAP. We could
well be expected to know, however, from published sourees
from Hutchison, and from the continued volume of personnery
in the Anti-Trust Seotion, that the SCAP people have not 
left the Japanese alone to shake the program down. I shouldO
think therefore that we could get out a wire, as suggested
by Marshall in his last paragraph, saying that we undorstand
that ohanges are being made in the Anti-Monopoly Law, tha
we assume these changes are part of the voluntary shake-
down called for by NS0 13/2, subjeot only to the provisio
that the fundamentals of the reform shall not be impaire
and that we would like coples of the proposed changes
purely for our information. This should get the point
across, which is about all we can do.
I agree with Marshall that it would be best not to
attempt to implement the last sentence of the·NSC paragraph
by defining "the nature and extent of the adjustments to be
permitted". It would just stir up a hornet's nest and
serve no useful purpose.

that body, as compared with the Anti-Trust Division, has
confined itselr to preservation of the essentials of the
program.

The NSC paragraph on the reform program recommends ink

DEPARTMENI OF STATE
Subject: Revision of Anti-Monopoly Law.
20
ui
8
The Honorable
894.602/11
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's telegram
No. 380 of November 2, 1948, and to this Mission's airgram No.
A-222 of November 8, 1948, and to forward a copy of a memoramdum
from the Japanese Fair Trade Commission to Mr. E. C. WELsH and
Mr. L. N. SALWIN of the Anti-Trust and Cartels Division, General
Headquarters, containing the Commission's latest proposals re-
garding revision of the Anti-Monopoly Law (Law No. 54 of 1947).
1848
As reported in the Mission's airgram, the Anti-Trust and
Cartels Section is most reluctant to furnish copies of prior
drafts, apparently feeling that unfavorable criticism may arise
from an airing of its differences with the Fair Trade Commission.
The present draft, however, is said by officials of the Division
to represent substantial agreement between General Headquarters
and the Japanese government, and may therefore be of interest to
the Department. It is reported that this draft will probably be
submitted to the present session of the Diet, provided that the.
Diet is not dissolved before such a step can be taken.
A/E
The principal revision, as reported previously, modifies
Article 10, regarding inter-corporate stock-holdings. The latest
C
0
JH
proposal by the Fair Trade Commission approaches the views held
by the Anti-Trust and Cartels Section, with the addition of a
third paragraph, which would allow inter-corporate holdings if
the combined effect of the two companies "would have an insignif-
icant effect on competition in any particular field of trade."
The latter provision is similar to one incorporated in Articles
4 and 6 of the unamended law. The Fair Trade Commission, it
will be noted, makes no mention of foreign firms in its latest
IAL FILE
draft of Article 10. The Commission had entered objections to
a proposal by the Anti-Trust and Cartels Division which would
specifically have allowed foreign firms to hold stock in one
Japanese company. Under the Commission's latest version, if
adopted, holding of stocks by foreign firms will presumably
depend
CONFIDENTIAL (FOR DEPT. USE ONLY)

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 734
November 18, 1948
-2
depend on the Commission's use of its discretionary powers,
particularly under Paragraph 3 permitting holdings when the ef-
fect on competition is inconsiderable.
Under the current proposal, Article 6 is to be revised to
provide for subsequent registration rather than advance approval
of international contracts by the Fair Trade Commission. In
Article ll, the clause limiting application of the Article to
companies with assets exceeding 5 million yen is to be deleted,
thus making the clause applicable to all companies; acquisition
by a company of more than 5 per cent of the stocks of another
company, hitherto prohibited, may be permitted by the Fair Trade
Commission following a showing by the company that the trans-
action does not restrict trade or in any other way violate the
provisions of paragraph 1 of Article 10. Article 12, limiting
inter-company holding of debentures, will be deleted entirely
if the current proposal is adopted.
Article 13, it is proposed, will be revised so that concur-
rent directorships will be prohibited in cases where inter-corpe-
rate stock-holdings would be prohibited under Paragraph 2 of
Article 10, the latter paragraph thereby being incorporatéd into
Article 13. The present limit on the number of directorships to
be held concurrently by any one person is to be revoked.
Article 14, under the present plan, will be amended to pro-
vide that no person may hold stocks in two competing companies if
his holdings in either of the two totals more than 10 per cent of
its stock. The provisions of the unamended law are ambiguous.
Article 15 is to be revised to require the advance approval of
the Fair Trade Commission only if a company contemplating a morger
has assets in excess of 10 million yen; other companies need only
report a merger to the Commission within twenty days. The provi-
sions of Article 15 will apply, mutatis mutandis, to Article 16.
A new paragraph is to be inserted into Article 17 to provide for
desist orders against violaters of Articles 7 to 16 inclusive.
The present proposal, representing a compromise between the
Anti-Trust and Cartels Division of General Headquarters and the
Japanese Fair Trade Commission, embraces considerably broader
changes in the law than had at first been contemplated by the
Commission. The views of the Anti-Trust and Cartels Division
appear to have prevailed in the latest draft of Articles 6 and
10, while Articles 12 and 13, under the current draft, represent
concessions to the Commission. As previously reported, the
interest evinced by the Deconcentration Review Board in the Anti-
Monopoly Law apparently emboldened the Japanese to suggest sweep-
ing revisions along the lines of American anti-trust legislation;
negotiations since September 25, when the most drastic proposal
was submitted, have resulted generally in turning the draft back
toward earlier and milder proposals, which had been considered
acceptable
CONFIDENTIAL (FOR DEPT. USE ONLY)

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.97)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 734
November 18, 1948
-3-
acceptable by the Japanese before the Deconcentration Review
Board's intervention.
It was stated in our airgram of November 8, 1948, referred
to above, that Mr. Edward C. Welsh, Chief of the Anti-Trust
and Cartels Division, would be in Washington for consultation
on about November 10. Mr. Welsh's trip, however, has been in-
definitely postponed.

W. J. Sebald

ARTICLE 4
2nd November 1948
1.
No entrepreneur shall participate in any one of the
following types of concerted activities:
1) establishment, stabilization or enhancement of prices;
2) restriction on volume of production or that of sales;
3) restrictions on technology, products, markets or
customers;
4) restrictions on construction or expansion of facilities
or on edoption of new technology or methods of production.
2.
The provisions of the preceding paragraph shall not apply
in case the effects of such concerted activities of competi-
tion within a particular field of trade are not substantial.
ARTICLE 6
2nd November 1948
1.
No entrepreneur shall participate in an international
agreement or an international contract with a foreign entrepreneur
or participate in an agreement or contract on foreign trade with a
domestic entrepreneur with regard to any agreement or contract
which contains therein any matter coming under any one (1) of the
Items of Paragraph 1 of Article 4.
2.
The provisions of the preceding paragraph shall not apply
in case the effects of such agreement or contract on competition
in any particular field of trade are not substantial.
3.
Every entrepreneur shall, when he has participated in an
international agreement or an international contract with a
foreign entrepreneur or an agreement or contract on foreign trade
with a domestic entrepreneur, which agreement or contract is to
continue for a considerable length of time (excluding such agree
ment or contract where the delivery of the object due to one (1)
transaction takes place over a considerable period of time or such
agreement or contract which merely provides for the appointment of
a foreign or domestic entrepreneur as the agent of the principal).
file a report with the Fair Trade Commission within twenty (20)
days of the day of participation in said agreement or contract.
ARTICLE 9
2nd November 1948
1.
No person shall establish a holding company or convert a
company into a holding company.
2.
The term "holding company" as used in the preceding para-
paragraph

ARTICLE 10
2nd November 1948
No company shall acquire or own stocks (axcluding those
without voting rights: hereinafter the same) of another company
where the effect of such acquisition or ownership may result in
a substantial restraint of trade and thereby be contrary to the
public interest, or where the acquisition is due to the empley-

2. No company whose business is other than financial shall acquire

1) a company engsged in competition with it in any particulan

2) a company engaged in competition with a company whose

3) a company engaged in competition with a third company,

quiring company.

The prohibitions of the preceding paragraph shall not apply

whose total assets (excluding unpaid-up capital stock, unpaid-up
partnership share or claim rights against unpaid-up fixed funds)
exceed five million yen (Y5,000,000) shall file a report with the
Fair Trade Commission within twenty (20) days of its having acquired
stocks of another company coming under any one (1) of the following
Items:
1) a company engaged in competition with it:
2) a company engaged in competition with a company whose
stocks are owned by it;
3) a company engaged in competition with a third company,
one or more of whose officers or employees are also officers
of the acquiring company;
4) a cogpany whose stocks are also owned by a company en-
gaged in competition with the acquiring company:
5) a company, one or more of whose officers or employees are
also officers of a third company engaged in competition with
the acquiring company;
6) a company which stands in continuous relation to it in
regard to supply of materials, services or other economic
benefits necessary for its business activities;
7) a company which owns stocks in a third company;
6.
Notwithstanding the provisions of the preceding paragraph,
every company principally engaged in buying and selling of goods
and whose total assets exceed five million yen (Y5,000,000) shall
file a report with the Fair Trade Commission within twenty (20)
days of its having acquired stocks of another company.
7.
The provisions of the foregoing two (2) paragraphs shall
apply to any company whose business is other than financial and
whose total assets come to exceed five million yen (Y5,000,000)
after the date of acquisition of stocks, in which event a report
thereof shall be promptly filed with the Fair Trade Commission.
ARTICLE 11
2nd November 1948
1.
No company whose business is financial shall acquire or
own stocks of another compeny with which it is engaged in competi-
tion and which operates in the same field of financial business.
2.

2. No company whose business is financial shall acquire or

the total issue of stock of said company unless permission of the
Fair Trade Commission has been obtained after making an affirmative
showing warranting a finding that said acquisition or owmership
does not violate the provisions of Paragraph 1 of the preceding
Article.
3.
The provisions of the preceding two (2) paragraphs shall
not apply to any one (1) of the following cases:
1) acquisition of stocks by a company engaged in the

2) acquisition of stocks by acceptance of a security trust
only wherein the trustor is the beneficiary and said trustor
exercises the voting right;
3) acquisition of stocks as the result of legitimate fore
closure or satisfaction of a lien or claim, or pursmant to a
final reorgenization plan approved in accordance with the
provisions of special law.
4. In case the period of ownership of stocks coming under
Item 1 or Item 3 of the preceding paragraph will exceed one (1)
year from the day of acquisition, prior permission af the Fair
Trade Commission shall be obtained.
ARTICLE 12
2nd November 1948
This Article shall be deleted in whole.
ARTICLE 13
3nd November 1948
1.
No officer or employee of a company shall hold concurrently
a position as an officer in another company where the effect thereof
may result in a substantial restraint of competition in any particu-
lar field of trade and thereby be contrary to the public interest,
or where the holding of such office concurrently is due to the
employment of unfair methods of competition.
2.
No officer or employee of a company shall concurrently hold
a position as an officer in another company coming under any one
(1) of the Items of Paragraph 2 of Article 10, which are hereby
incorporated by reference.
3.
The provisions of Paragraph 3 of Article 10 shall apply
mutatis mutandis to such cases as come under the preceding para-
graph.

ARTICLE 14
2nd November 1948
No person (excluding a company; hereinafter the same for

the effect of such acquisition or ownership may result in a sub-
stantial restraint of competition in any particular field of trade
and thereby be contrary to the public interest.

of said companies, file a report with the Fair Trade Commission

ARTICLE 15
2nd November 1948
1.
No company shall effect a merger in any one (1) of the
following cases, thereby being contrary to the public interest:
1) where substantial disparities in bargaining power will
arise due to the merger;
2) where the merger may cause a substantial restraint of
competition in any particular field of trade;
3) where the merger has been coerced by unfair methods of
competition.
2.
No company whose total assets exceed ten million yen
(Y10,000,000) shall be a party to a merger unless permission of the
Fair Trade Commission has been obtained after making an affirmative
showing warranting a finding that said merger will not come under
any one (1) of the Items of the preceding paragraph.
3.
With the exception of such cases coming under the preceding
paragraph, every company shall, when it has effected a merger, file
a report

a report with the Fair Trade Commission within twenty (20) days of

ARTICLE 16
2nd November 1948
1.
The provisions of the preceding Article shall be applied
mutatis mutandis to such cases where a company receives transfer
of the whole or a part of the business of another company. it r
ceives transfer of a substantial portion of the fixed assets of
another company, it leases the whole or substantial portion of the
business of another company, receives entrustment of the whole or
substantial portion of the management of another company or it
enters into a contract which provides for a joint profit and loss
account with another company; provided that "merger" shall read as
"receive transfer of the whole or a part of the business of another
company, receive transfer of a substantial portion of the fixed
assets of another company, lease the whole or substantial portion
of the business of another company, receive entrustment of the whole
or substantial portion of the management of another company or enter
into a contract which provides for a joint profit and loss account
with another company."
ARTICLE 17-2
2nd November 1948
1.
In case there exists an act in violation of the provisions
of Article 9, Article 10, Article 11, Article 15, Article 16 or
Article 17, the Fair Trade Commission may order the entrepreneur
concerned, in accordance with the procedures as provided for in
Section 2 of Chapter 8, to take any measure necessary for eliminating
such violation.
2.
In case there exists an act in violation of the provisiono of
Article 13 or Article 14, the Fair Trade Commission may order said
violater to take any necessary measure for eliminating such violation.
3.
The procedure provided for by Section 2 of Chapter 8 shall
be applied mutatis mutandis to such cases as provided for by the
preceding paragraph; provided the terms "entrepreneur" and "said
entrepreneur" shall read as "said violater".

Subject: Anti-Trust and Deconcentration Policy.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
894002/9-2048
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to our despatches No. 619 of
04.602/11-2248
Board", and No. 695 of October 28, 1948,. "Anti-Trust and Deconcen-
tration Policy", and to report recent developments in the applice-
tion of the Law for the Elimination of Excessive Concentrations of
Economic Power (Law No. 207 of 1947).
On November 5, the Japanese Holding Company Liquidation Com-
mission, acting upon the findings of the Deconcentration Review
Board, cancelled the designations for reorganization of the Nippon
Soda Company and the Nissan Chemical Industrial Company (Nissan
Kagaku Kogyo K. K.). On November 18, the Commission cancelled the
designations of the Kobe Steel Company (Kobe Seikosho K. K.), the
Japan Steel Tube Company (Nippon Kokan K. K.), and the Fuso Metal
Industry Company (Fuso Kinzoku Kogyo K. K.), as well as the desig-
nations of 142 of the 175 companies which had previously been re
leased from designation for reorganization but which must nonethe-
1/
less dispose of intercorporate stock holdings. A list of the 142
companies affected is enclosed. As reported in our despatch No.
695 of October 28, 1948, the latter cases will remain in the hands
of the Japanese Fair Trade Commission for action under the Anti-
Monopoly Law (Law No. 54 of 1947), and stock disposal remains man-
datory unless, as seems likely, the latter law is revised by the
0
Diet.
2
There are thus 33 companies still designated for minor reor-
ganisation and for stock disposal; the Japanese press appears o-
+#
fident, however, that they will be freed in the near future. In
addition, there are still 95 companies designated for structural
reorganization; of these, 4l are now under review by the Holding
Company Liquidation Commission, and, since they seem to represent
cases of lesser concentration than those of the Wippon Soda Company
and Nissan Kagaku, it appears probable that they will be removed
from
CONFIDENTIAL (FOR DEPT. USE ONLY)

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo'. Despatch No. 741
November 22, 1948
-2
from designation at the Commission's next meeting. scheduled for
early December. The Nihon Keizai Shimbun, which has been remark-
ably successful in predicting new developments in deconcentration
policy, has concluded that in the final analysis only the Japan
Iron and Steel Company (Nippon Sei tetsu K. K.). the Japan Mmpress
Company (Nippon Tsuun K. K.), the Oji Paper Company (Oji Seishi
K. K.), the Dainippon Brewing Company (Dainippon Biiru K. K.), the
Japan Electric Generation and Transmission Company (Nippon Hasseden
K. K.). and the Hokkaido Dairy Farm Cooperative (Hokkaido Rakumo
Kyodo K. X.) will be required to reorganize.
Whether indeed any reorganizations will take place will depend
on the Deconcentration Review Board's findings in the Japan Iron
and Steel and Oji Paper cases. The Board is at present in Kyushu
studying the facilities of Japan Iron and Steel, and should reach
a decision on both cases shortly.
Whether or not orders for reorganisation are finally drafted
in the few cases still considered eligible by the Nihon Keisei. it
becomes apparent from the Board's decisions in the five reorgani-
zation cases recently dismissed that application of the Deconcen-
tration Law has been effectively circumscribed. Though General
Headquarters maintains that there has been no change in policy.
the substitution of the four principles announced by the Deconcen-
tration Review Board in September for the standards origimally set
up under the Deconcentration Law has in effect amended that law.
The swiftness with which action is likely to be taken in suc-
ceeding cases is indicated by the fact that public hearings, though
scheduled, have never been held in the cases of the three steel
companies whose designations were recently cancelled. In the case
of Nippon Soda, hearings were held and a final order transmitted to
the company by the Holding Company Liquidation Commission, which
order was subsequently stayed by the Deconcentration Review Board;
in the case of Nissan Kagaku, the hearings were held, but, pending
announcement of the Board's findings, no order wes drafted; and
finally, in the cases of the three steel companies, the Board's
findings were transmitted to the Holding Company Liquidation Com-
mission before the hearings were held. The proceedings are thus
becoming more and more abbreviated, and the Board is making its
wishes known at an increasingly early stage. It is not unlikely
that mass releases from designation will occur in the future even
prior to the drafting of preliminary orders by the Holding Company
Liquidation Commission.
The final reorganization order transmitted to Nippon Soda on
August 20, and the recent order cancelling the designation, issued
2/3
on November 5, are enclosed for the information of the Department.
The former is the only final order for reorganization that has thus
far been prepared by the Holding Company Liquidation Comission.
Coming before the enunciation of the four basic principles by the
Deconcentration Review Board, it made no attempt to establish a
prima facie
CONFIDENTIAL (FOR DEPT. USE ONLY)

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 741
November 22, 1948
-3-
prima facie case of restraint of trade. If final orders are
drafted in the future, they will undoubtedly contain a clmmse
indicating that such a case hes been established.
The November 5 order, it will be noted, is at pains to point
out that it in no way prejudices further investigation by the
Fair Trade Commission under the Anti-Monopoly Law; it is thus con-
ceivable that a reorganization could be ordered by the latter
Commission, though such an eventuality seems most unlikely in
view of the interest the Deconcentration Review Board has shown
in projects for softening the Anti-Monopoly Law.
Also enclosed as of possible interest to the Department are
typical specimens of briefs filed with the Holding Company Liquida-
tion Commission prior to public hearings on the casè of Nissen
Kagaku, which hearings were held on October 22. The brief filed
41
by non-union labor in the Chemical Department (copy enclosed ) and
that filed jointly by the Oil and Fat Union and the Paint Union
5/
(copy enclosed) are typical of the objections to deconcentration
that have been consistently voiced by labor. It has been pointed
out by the Anti-Trust and Cartels Division, General Headquarters,
that the powerful opposition of the unions may make reorganisation
impossible after release from designation, even if, as seems likely
in the present cases, the companies themselves may consider reor-
ganization necessary for the sake of business efficiency.
Identical briefs were filed by the President of Nissan Kagaku
and by the Chairman of the Congress of Nissan Chemical Unions. A
6/
copy of the brief submitted by the President of Nissan Kagaku is
enclosed. The objections enunciated in the two briefs seem to re
volve chiefly around a fear that a stigma may be attached to desig-
nation as an excessive concentration under the Deconcentration
Review Board's four principles; "we cannot afford to expose our-
selves to such a reproach, without any prima facie case, it is
noted in both briefs.
It is thus possible that Nissan Kagaku and Nippon Soda, though
objecting to reorganization under Law No. 207 and subsequent addi-
tions to that law by the Deconcentration Review Board, may desire
71
to reorganize independently. The enclosed translation of an artf-
cle from the Asahi Shimbun of November 5 is typical of several
that have recently predicted such a development. The Japanese
press, including the conservative Asahi and Mainichi, has been
highly critical of compenies that have attempted to maintain over-
extended enterprises, pointing to Showa Denko as an example of
the vices and maladjustments that may result, and has frequently
noted the necessity of wholesale reorganisation for the sake of
efficiency. Whether such reorganization will be possible without
ScAP backing would appear to depend chiefly on the labor unions
and their powers of resistance.
Respectfully yours,
W. 'J. Sebald

HOLDING COMPANY LIQUIDATION COMMISSION
In the Matter of:
Nippon Sode Kabushiki Keisha)
HCLC No. 172
(Nippon Soda Company. Ltd.)
FINAL ORDER
Approving Voluntary
Designated under Art. 3 of Law
Reorganization Plan
o. 207 of 1947
The Holding Compeny Liquidation Commission, hereinafter
referred to as the "Commission", having held a public hearing
on the Proposed Order on 10 August 1948 pursuant to the pro-
visions of Article 9 of Law No. 207 of 1947: Law for the
Elimination of Excessive Concentrations of Economic Power,
ell pursuant to proper notice to the designee Nippon Soda
Kabushiki Kaisha, hereinafter referred to es "Nippon," to
the duly authorized representatives of Nippon's employees,
to all other persons filing notices of appearance, as set
forth in the affidavit of publication and service of notice
attached hereto; the Commission having heard and considered
such objections, recommendations, end statements as were
submitted; the Commission having transmitted to the Fair
Trade Commission said Proposed Order and having received no
indication of conflict with the provision of Law No. 54 of
1947: Law relating to Prohibition of Private Monopoly and
Methods of Preserving Fair Trade; and Nippon having elected
to establish Nisshin having elected to establish Nisshin
Koko K.K., end Tohoku Kozan K.K., as separate companies
rather than combine them into a single company as permitted
in the Proposed Order:
NOW THEREFORE, the Commission orders that:
1. The Proposed Order in this matter, dated 22 July
1948, is confirmed in every respect and is hereby declared
end decreed to be final, with the exception that the provision
of Article 17 thereof permitting a combination of the above
mentioned companies is deleted in accordance with Nippons
election, a copy of which Proposed Order as modified is
attached hereto and made a part hereof.
2. All Nippon's creditors whose claims arose between 8
February 1948 and the date of this Final Order desiring to
have their claims stipulated in accordance with the provisions
of Article 5 of Law No. 208 of 1947 shall report such claims
to the Commission within 15 days of the dete of this Final
Order, setting forth the dates when such claims arose, the
nature thereof, their original amounts and unpaid balances.

Reorganization Plan
A
JURISDICTION
1. The Holding Compeny Liquidation Commission, hereinafter
referred to as the "Commission," having designated Nippon Soda
Kabushiki Kaisha, hereinafter referred to as "Nippon" as an ex-
cessive concentration of economic power on 8 February 1948,
pursuant to the powers granted to it by virtue of the provision
of Article 3 and in accordance with the standards adopted under
the provisions of Article 6 of Law No. 207 of 1947: nimination
of Excessive Concentrations of Economic Power Law: Nippon having
entered its appearance to the proceedings by the filing of for-
mal notice pursuant to Article 14 of the Rules of Procedure
issued under said Law; having filed its statement with respect
to the applicability of Section B of the Standards for Excessive
Concentretions in Industrial Fields adopted pursuant to said Law,
its intention to prepare a reorganization plan and its completed
questionnaire, all within 30 days after designation pursuant to
Article 16 of the Rules of Procedure; having filed a voluntary
reorganization plan and a statement as to the epplicability of
Section C of the same Stanoards, all within 60 days after
designation pursuant to Article 17 of said Rules; and said
voluntary reorganization plan as amended having been submitted
to the Commission for its epproval pursuant to the provisions
of item 7, paragraph 2 of Article 7 of the Law.
B
THE PLAN OF REORGANIZATION
2. And whereas Nippon in said reorganization plan provides
for the elimination of its concentration of excessive economic
power by the establishment of six independent and separate com-
penies, the appointment of independent and non-interlocking
directorates, the transfer of certain assets and liabilities to
each new company, and the dissolution of Nippon; representing.in
such plan that each new company will commence business in a
solvent condition capable of efficient production in a competitive
market.

C
ORDER
15. NOW, THEREFCRE, the Commission, having considered
all stetements, documents, end recommendations filed by all
parties to the proceedings, end the voluntary reorganization
plan hereinbefore recited, the Commission now finds and orders:
16. That Nippon is e private enterprise conducted for
profit which by reason of relative size in its principal line
of business and the cumuletive power of its position in many
lines, restricts competition and impairs the opportunity of
others to engage in business independently in important segments
thereof; and is therefore an excessive concentration of economic
power within the meening of Law No. 207 of 1947: Elimination
of Excessive Concentrations of Economic Power Law.
17. That the voluntary reorganization plan filed by Nippon,
the provisions of which are recited in Section B of this Order,
will eliminate the excessive concentration of economic power in
the interest of public welfare and is hereby approved, and Nippon
and the new companies provided for therein are hereby ordered and
directed to carry out and abide by such plan and each of its pro-
visions.
18. That the plan of reorganization approved under the
provisions of Law No. 40 of 1946 to be submitted pursuant to
Nippon's voluntary reorganization plan as set forth in Article
13 above, shall become a part of this Order and Nippon and th
new companies are hereby ordered and directed to carry out and
abide by said plan of reorganization as approved.
19. That Nippon shall execute the transfers to the new
companies in accordance with the provisions of the approved
plan subject only to the jurisdiction of the Commission; and
all documents and instruments of transfer and all other documents,
instruments and public notices required to carry out this Order
shall be submitted to the Commission for its prior approval.
20. That

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 741
November 22, 1948.
(8)
20. That the new companies provided for in the plan shall
in no way violate the provisions of Law No. 54 of 1947: Law.
Relating to Prohibition of Private Monopoly and Methods of Pre-
serving Fair Trade; nor shall the approval of the plan be con-
strued to authorize or sanction any such violation; nor shall
such approval be construed to limit in any way the power and
authority granted to the Commission by virtue of the provisions
of Imperial Ordinance No. 233 of 1946 pursuant to which Nippon
has been designated as a holding compeny for dissolution.
21. For the purpose of supervising execution of this order,
any duly authorized representative of the Commission or its
successor, on reasonable notice to Nipnon or any one of the new
companies, their successors or assigns, shall be allowed (1)
reasonable access to their books, ledgers, accounts, correspondence,
memoranda and other records and documents (2) to interview their
officers or employees, who may have counsel present, and (3)
request reports as may from time to time be reasonably necessary
for the execution of this Order.
22. Jurisdiction of this procee ing is retainedfor the
purpose of enabling any interested party to apply to the Commission
or its successor for such further orders or directions as may be
appropriate for the construction or execution of this Order, and
for the amendment, modification or termination of any of the pro-
visions thereof, and for punishment of violations thereof.

HOLDING COMPANY LIQUIDATION COMMISSION
Date: 5 November 1948
In the Matter of:
HCLC No. 172
Nippon Soda Kabushiki Kaisha
(Nippon Soda Company, Ltd.)
ORDER
Designated under Art. 3 of
OF CANCELLATION
Law No. 207 of 1947
OF DESIGNATION
In order to effect speedy disposition of matters before it
pursuant to the provisions of Law No. 207 of 1947: Elimination
of Excessive Concentrations of Economic Power Law, and not having
readily available such evidence as will establish a prima facie
case of restraint of trade in this Matter, the Holding Company
Liquidation Commission does hereby order:
1. That the designation of Nippon Soda Kabushiki Kaisha,
in accordance with said Law be and the same is hereby cancelled,
and the Final Order in this Matter issued on 20 August 1948 and
all other orders and instructions issued in this matter be and
the same in hereby revoked.
2. That such cancellation of designation and revocation
of order and instructions shall become effective as of the date
of this Order and shall have no retroactive effect.
3. That this Order shall have no effect on rights of
preference of creditors who have claims on loans which have
arisen for business between the date of designation and 20
August 1948, as provided for in Article 5 of Law No. 208 of
1947. All creditors filing appropriate claims for preference
within fifteen (15) days after the date of the Final Order
hereinbefore referred to shall be granted such preference by
stipulation in separate order.
4. That this Order shall not be construed as relieving
Nippon Soda Kabushiki Kaisha from compliance with any provision of
Law No. 40 of 1946, Imperial Ordinance No. 657 of 1945, Imperial
Ordinance No. 233 of 1946, and all other applicable laws,
regulations and orders of the Japanese Government; nor shall this
Order be construed as a prohibition against the establishment of
second companies under and by virtue of the provisions of said
Law No. 40 of 1946.

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 3
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 741
November 22, 1948.
- 2 -
5. That the Official Records in this Matter (or official
copies thereof) and all information and evidence, be and the same
are hereby delivered and transferred to the Fair Trade Commission.
6. That this order shall not be construed as relieving
Nippon Soda Kabushiki Kaisha from compliance with all provisions
of Law No. 54 of 1947 or as a finding that it has not undertaken
any restraint of trade, affected a private monopolization. employed
unfair methods of competition, possessed undue substantial
disparities in bargaining power, or committed other acts contre-
vening Chapters 2 or 4 of said Law, or is not now doing so; and
shall be without prejudice to any investigation or course of
action which the Fair Trade Commission or the Publie Procurator
General shall comence or to any decision, judgment or other
ruling which said Conmission or the High Court of Tokyo may render
under said Law: nor shall it constitute a defense to or evidence
in refutation of charges, if any, made in any proceedings of the
Fair Trade Commission or the High Court of Tokyo undertaken pur-
suant to the provisions of said Law No..54 of 1947, or other
orders or regulations thereunder, or of Law No. 191 of 194s.

In accordance with the four principles, which were suggested
by the "Deconcentration Board" on September 1l, 1948, to be follew-
ed in implementing Public Law No. 207, the Deconcentration Law, the
Non-labour-union-members of the Chemical Depertment of the Nissan
Kagaku Kogyo K.K., have carefully investigated the "Six Company
Reorganization Plan" already submitted to your office, whether it
shows "a prima facie" in the above four principles.
It came to a conclusion that we should declare in the nega-
tive as a result of the following two (2) reasons!
1) There is no case that our company has ever restricted
the free competition, or impaired the opportunity for
others to engage in business independently in the same
field of our business, although our productive capacity
of lime superphosphate is deened comparatively large.
2)
Our company has never had such a big scale of product-
ion as enables us to operate the market independently
by ourselves.
Therefore, we hereby made an objection to the proposed
order issued on October 2, 1948, in compliance with the
Article 35, Chapter XIII of the Rules of Procedure under
the Elimination of Excessive Concentration of Economic
Power Law.

Brief of abiection not to be conaidered

1. In accordance with the Law No. 207 issued 1947, on Elimine-

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 6
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 741
November 22, 1948.
-2-
Brief of abjection not to be considered
an excessive concentration of economic
pozer
1.
Standards for Excessive Concentration in Industrial Fields
(notified on Feb. 8th, 194s) showed us five (5) criteria
as the standards for excessive concentrations of economic
power.
2. After careful examination, we concluded that our company
could not be considered an excessive concentration of
oconomic power within the above criteria.
3. But in contemplation of the criteria we had some doubts in
our comparatively large production capacity of lime
superphosphate, in our rather large-scale management,
combining five business fields which have little relatiom
with one another, and in the fact that superphosphate and
sulphate of ammonia are produced in separate factories
though they are both fertilizers but do not have any close
relation in production process. Therefore, we temporarily
assumed that we probably should be considered an excessive
concentration.
Hence we planned out the reorganisation plan to set up
Six new companies.
5.
This reorganization plan had been coming to a conclusion
after several negotiations with the H. C. J. C. So we
submitted the nearly concluded plan (the amended reorgani-
zation plan) dated Sept. 5th, 1948 to the H. C. L. C.
6.
The proposed order dated Oct. 2nd was based on the preceding
reorganization plan.
7.
The G. H. Q. authorities, however reported on Sept. 12th
four interpretive provisions to be conformed in implementing
the Deconcentration Law.
8.
. The above provisions tells us that the mere possession of
non-related lines of business is not in itself sufficient
in any case to establish that a company is in excessive
concentration within the law and also that no order should
be issued under the law unless there is a showing of a
prima facie case that the company restricts competition or
impairs the opportunity for others to engage in business
independently in any important segment of business.
9.
At this report, we found ourselves missing the meaning of
the criteria to the effect that our company was an excessive
concentration of economic power.

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 6
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 741
November 22, 1948.
(3)
10. If the proposed order dated Oct. 2nd should come to the
final order, our company must be reproached of the prime
facie cases written in the four provisions. We cannet
afford to expose ourselves to such a reproach, without
any prima facie case.
11. Our production capacity is comparatively large but it is
not due to say that our company restricts competition or
impair the opportunity for others to engage in our busimess.
There should be many other factors till production capacity
reflects on competition.
12. Our company is not managed on such a large scale as to
control market prices at our will. There are many other
powerful fellow mamufacturers and moreover there are many
substitutes.
We were forced to take part in cartels under strong
pressures of keen competition, but we did not always pros-
per by so doing. The competition between the cartel and
outsiders in the superphosphate industry is notable in the
history of the fertilizer industry of our country.
13. Our company has had no special priority or route to acquire
raw materials. We cannot, even though we wish, beat down
competitors by means of manipulation in acquisition of raw
materials. On the contrary, we are afraid that we might be
beaten down by their manipulation. Taking an example in
pyrite, a raw material of fertilizer, the production would
be stopped entirely if the supply of pyrite were out down
from big pyrite mines such as Yanahara and Matsuo. As a
raw material of soap, we can manufecture hardened oil so
much as consume, therefore, we might have no need to be
afraid of such manipulation. But it goes without saying
that the hardened oil industry ought to be based on acquisi-
tion of cheap and ebundant fish oil. Nowadays it is hard
to get and in high price. We are in difficulties due to a
large amount of mintenance and operation expenses fer
hardened oil plants of low efficiency. In the future when
we can enter into the international market, the prices of
soap and such other products are to be completely dependent
on and not contrary to the up-and-down of the fish oil prices
which are overwhelmed with the changes in the world-wide
market of general fat and oil. So the capacity of our
company will be negligibly feeble to control the prices.
During the war-time our company had joined the various con-
trolling associations in order to get allocation of raw
materials and had been share-holders of the sales controlling
corporations in order to sell the products. It was only
because it was impossible to continue the business without
entering them under the controlled economy.

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 6
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 741
November 22, 1948.
(4)
We are now disposing of the shares which we held and
all these controlling institutes have already lost their
functions. We shall in future have no chance to take
advantage of them.
15. There were many cases for us to expand the business
scale in the form of amalgamation or purchase. In almost
all the cases the amalgamations or purchases were done by
the enterprisers standing on the verge of bankruptey in
order to help each other in bad days. The chemical depart-
ment of the Japan Mining Co. was purchased by the Japan Fat
and Oil Co., in April 1945. The former was in trouble owing
to low-efficiency operation due to stoppage of supply of
foreign phosphate rock, an important raw material of
fertilizer. It was decided to be combined with the latter
in order to improve the business in co-relation between
organic and inorganic chemical industries and to maintain
its peace-time industry in the war time.
It is not too much to say that the amalgamations and pur-
chases in the past time were only protective measures.
16.
These measures have no influence to monopolize the market,
therefore, there are many cases of new enterprisems to
enter into our business field.
17. We cannot find any fact that our company had restricted
competition or kept others from entering our business field.
18. We, hereby, make objection against the proposed order which
notified us that our company should be considered an excessive
concentration and it must be eliminated for public good.
Postscript.
It is stated in theproposed order dated October 2nd, 1948.
that the loan of approximately ¥50,000,000 due from the Reconstruo-
tion Financing Bank shall not be succeeded to the new company but
shall be kept by the old company.
As we have previously asked the General Headquarters and
H.C.L.C., we earnestly wish that the above loan would be succeeded
to the new company, on account of the special circumstances, whether
or not our company be determined to fall under the category of
excessive concentration.
NISSAN CHEMICAL IND. CO.,LTD.
/a/ S. Kurobe
/t/ (Sadao Kurobe)
President & Director

(Full Translation)
DESIGNATION OF TWO COMPANIES CANCELLED
- Asahi - 5 November 1948.-
The Holding Company Liquidation Commission held an extra-
ordinary general meeting yesterday to re-examine, on the basis
of four principles, the Economic Deconcentration Law with res-
pect to the Nihon Soda and Nissan Kagaku (chemical) Companies.
to which the commission had issued instructions to reorganise
into six new companies.
As a result, instructioms to cancel the previous designations
will be issued to the two companies as of today because no olear
evidence testifying to any restriction on competition has been
found up to the present.
The companies will thus be excluded from the application of
the aforementioned law, and the cancellation of designation will
become effective on and after the date of the instruction cancell-
ing designations. Accordingly, the preferential right of the
creditor of loans, provided for in "The Law eencerning the special
cases of the Enterprise Reconstruction and Adjustment Law to supple
ment the execution of the Economic Deconcentration Law," shall not
be modified (the law stipulates that in the case of a low advanced
to a company subsequent to its receipt of the designation in accord-
ance with the Economic Deconcentration Law, the creditor has the
preferential right to be repaid the loan).
These companies will not be excluded from the application of
the Enterprise Reconstruction and Adjustment Law, Restricted Company
Ordinance, Company Security Holding Restriction Ordinance and the
Antimonopoly Law. The formal records, data and other documents
relating to these companies will be turned over to the Fair Trans-
actions Commission.
It is thought likely that the Nihon Soda Company will be
divided into three - companies,chemical, iron and steel -- and
mining and the Nissan Kagaku Company will likewise be reorganised
into three -- oil and gunpowder, chemical fertiliser, and paints.
