—OONPIDMNTIAL
SALE OF ZAIBATSU SECURITIES IN JAPAN
Sales to the Japanese public of Zaibatsu securities, including
those formerly held by the Zaibatsu families and the Zaibatsu holding
companies, have been conducted by the Securities Coordinating Liquidation
Committee (SCLC) for the Holding Company Liquidation Commission (HCLC).
The latter agency is responsible for accepting transfer of securities
owned by the Zaibatsu and for liquidating these securities; the former
is responsible for their actual disposal to the public. Data available
in Washington are conflicting, and it is therefore difficult to answer
with reasonable certainty the following questions on the status of
liquidation and sale of these securities:
1. What is the total number and value of securities (by
type, i.e., stocks and bonds) formerly held by
Zaibatsu families and ultimately subject to disposal
by sale to the public through the SCLC, as of the
most recent date?
2. Whet is the total number and value of Zaibatsu family
securities (by type) that have been transferred to
the SCLC, as of the most recent date?
3. What is the total number and value of Zaibatsu family
securities (by type) that have been sold by the SCLC
to the public, as of the most recent date?
-CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.99)
4. What is the status of seourities held by Zaibatsu
holding companies in respect to questions 1 through
3 above?
Such data as are available are given in the attached table. An
attempt is made in the following paragraphs to summarize and evaluate
the pertinent data.
Securities Subject to Liquidation
The total number of securities ultimately to be liguidated by the
SCLC is reported by the Foreign Broadcast Information Branch (FBIB) at about
162,000,000 shares, plus a relatively mall number of bonds. It is estimated
by DRF that the bonds would not bring the total above 170,000,000. This
and
total includes securities of 10 Zaibatsu families/83 Zaibatsu holding companies.
The figure of 170,000,000 is believed to be more acceptable than that of
189,000,000 reported earlier by an unofficial SCAP source. It is also:
believed that the total value of 7,533,000,000 yen reported recently by
the FBIB for these securities is more acceptable that either the high
figure of 8,235,000,000 yen reported earlier by an unofficial SCAP source
or the figure of 6,900,000,000 yen reported previously in a SCAP Summation.
Securities Transferred to the SCLC
The number of securities transferred to the SCLC as of November 20,
1948 may be estimated at not more than 153,000,000, including transfers
of shares reported in the FBIB and the number of bonds transferred as
estimated by DRF from value data given in the FBIB. The paid-up value
of these securities is estimated at about 6,670,000,000 yen. No data
other than those given by the FBIB are available.
CONPIDENTIAL

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.99)
Soouritios Sold by the SCLC
Two sources report numbers and value of securities sold es of
November 20, 1948. The FBIB reports that 23,657,000 shares have been
sold; inoluding bonds estimated by DRF on the basis of values reported by
the FBIB, the total number of securities sold probably would not exeeed
23,800,000. The FBIB reports the total value of securities sold as of
November 20, 1948 at 1,040,000,000.yen. An official SCAP report states
that, as of June 30, 1948, securities sold totaled 8,955,195 shares valued
shorter period covered by the official SCAP report, the value data reported
by these two sources are in reasonable agreement; the number of seourities
reported by the FBIB, however, even with allowance for the difference in
the period covered, is about double that reported officially by SCAP. A
more acceptable figure is believed to be that recently obtained from a
reliable SCAP source. This source stated that about 33,446,000 shares,
valued at 2,133,000,000 yen, had been sold as of November 20, 1948; no data
were given with respect to bond sales. This figure is roughly in agreement
with a statement made by Edward C. Welsh, Chief of the Anti-Trust and Cartels
Division of SCAP, on June 30, 1948 that "nearly 2 billion yen worth of
securities in former Zaibatsu companies have been sold."

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.98)
OONPIDTIAL
Relationship Between Value of Sales and Total Value of Beourities to be
Liguidated
In addition to the diffioulties in the data noted above, it is
not clear how the securities to be liquidated by and those already
transferred to, the SCLC are valued. Furthermore, it is not clear how
their reported values relate to the reported value of liquidated
securities. Thus it is difficult to relate value of sales to the value
of securities ultimately to be liquidated by or already transferred to,
the SCLC. On the assumption that the total value of securities to be
liquidated is about 7,532,000,000 yen and the total value of securities.
transferred to the SCLC is about 6,669,000,000 yen, the FBIB - reported
sales of 1,O40,000,000 yen of Zaibatsu securities by the SCLC would be
equal to about 14 percent of the total to be liquidated and 16 percent
of the total transferred to the SCLC. On the other hand, if the figure
of 2, 133,000,000 yen for sales of Zaibatsu securities, reported by an
unofficial SCAp source, is accepted as correct, sales of securities by
the SCLC would be equal to about 28 percent of the total Zaibatsu
securities to be liquidated and 32 percent of the total value of
Zaibatsu securities transferred to the SCLC.
-CONFIDENTIAL

THE AMERICAN CHAIBER OF COMI-ERCE IN J
TOKYO
Report of the Sub-Committee on Business Iavestnonts
of the Fair Trade Practices Committee
American Chamber of Commerce In Japan
I. General
A number of Anerican corporetions which in pre-war years had
ly substantial investments in Japanese industry have sent represent
tives to Japan to investigate the desirability of renewing such in
vestments, of reestablishing patent and technique contracts, and or
providing engineering assistance, machinery and raw materials to for
ner Japanese associatec companies. In most instances, it was found
that Japanese net ods and techniques were from 1o to 15 years behind
those of the United States, that machinery was old and worn out, r
that tho source of raw materials was inadequate, enphasizing the me
of Japan for those services which American business is best in a po
sition to provide.
Unfortunately, however, existing laws, ordinances and regulation
have discouraged the return of Anerican business interests to Japan.
In the light of the recent announcement by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers declaring a change of policy to place primary
emphasis upon the economic recovery of Japan, this Sub-Committee has
undertaken to reviev some of the major laws and ordinances affecting
business pointing out those features which, in its opinion, have de-
terred the return of Anerican business, and reccmmending revisions
which it is felt will remove such deterrents.
II Principal Laws Affecting Business Deconcentration
Under this law and its related laws and ordinances, companies are
designated as "excessive concentrations of economic power" by a Gov-
ernment board called the Holding Company Liquidation Commission. Not
only are such designations made under principles, plans and procedures
determined by the HCLC itself, but the HCLC is also directed to 'elimi-
nate such excessive concentrations in the interest of public welfare",
and is empowered to order the dissolution of organizations. To accom-
plish its deconcentration nission HCLC is empowered to require of .
designated companies, their subsidiaries (defined for the purpose of
this law as conpanies 1/lo of whose stock is owmed by a designated
company), and affiliates (those companies 1/1o of vhose stock is owned
hy a subsidiary company), that they turn over to HCLc for disposition
all shares of stock owned by them, together with voting proxies. HCIC
is further directed to dispose of the stocks irrespective of the pro-
visions of other laws, ordinances, articles of incorporation, or con-
tracts, first to enployees of the company issuing the stock and then,
vith certain exceptions, to individuals residing within the field of
the companies' principai operations.
The procedure followed in establishing a price for the dispositin
of shares is controlled largely by the market price or, in the absenco
of such, by vhat buyers may be willing to pay, although other factors
also have a bearing.
This law and its related ordinances place in the hands of a gov-

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
ernment agency, the HCLC, the power to designate and eliminate
sive concentrations of economic power, an excessive concentratio
being defined for the purpose of this law as "any private enterprise
canducted for profit, or combination of such enterprises, which by
reason of its relative size in any line or the cumulative power of
its position in many lines, restricts competition or impairs the op-
portunity for others to engage in business independently, in any im-
portant segnent of business."
Although the law purports to establish certain so-called stan-
dards on vhich HCLC is supposed to base its designations, they are in
effect merely factors for the consideration of HCLC and in no case do
they establish any criteria from which it can be determined thet av
company is or is not an excessive concentration. Such detcrmination
is left almost completely to the whin of HCLC, and having made such
designation HCLC is also empowered to order the dissolution of organi-
zations and the termination of monopolistic or restrictive arrange-
ments, to require the submission of plans for reorganization and the
disposal of assets, and to take such other measures as it may deem
necessary to eliminate excessive concentrations of economic power.
(Law for Elimination of Excessive Concentrations of Econonic
Pover, No. 207, Gazette No. 517, December 18, 1947;
Law for Reconstruction and Reorganization of Economy in Conse-
quence of Enforcement of Law for Deconcentration of Economic
Power, Ho. 208, Gazette No. 517, December 18, 1947;
Securities Disposal Law, No. 8, Gazette 239, January 18, 1947;
Organization of Holding Company Liquidation Commission, Ord.
No. 233, April 20, 1946;
Restrictions on Possessing Securities, Ord. No. 567, November
25, 1946, as amended by Imp. Ord. 48, Gazette No. 260, Febru-
ary 13, 1947;
Law for Dividend of Profits and Others of Corporations, No. 190,
Gazette 1o. 515, December 16, 1947;
Law amending HCLC Ordinance, 1o. 204, Gazette No. 517, December
18,19+7;
Enforcement Regulations of HCLC Ordinance as revised by Minis-
terial Ord. No. 1, Gazette No. 355, June 9, 1947.)
Anti-Monopoly Lav
This law is closely related to the Deconcentration Law and is de-
siged to prohibit private monopolies, unreasonable restraints of
trade and unfair competition. The agency established in this case is
the Fair Trade Commission, which is empowered to order the cessation
of any of the prohibited acts and is also empowered to break up a
business vhere disparities in bargaining power are to be eliminated.
This law also prohibits holding companies and prohibits any company,
with a few cxceptions, from holding the stock of another corporation
egardless of percentage of ownership. It also prohibits the officers
r employecs of one company fron holding concurrently a position as an
ficer in another company if the companies are in competition, and
rovides for liability in damages to persons injurea by any private
onopoly, restraint of trade, or unfair competition irrespective of
ilfulness or negligence. All contracts in violation of the provis-
ons of tnis law are nullified.
-2-

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
Again, lile the Deconcentration Law, this hawag
mental agency broad powers to control business with
tations. It abounds with vague and general criteriaml
ves to almost any interpretation. For example, the ter
stantial disparities in bargaining power" is defined tomnol
ations where an entrepreneur controls a business so as ton
extremely difficult for another entrepreneur to start a new ont
prise. The term "unfair methods of of competition" is definedst
clude unwarranted refusal to receive or supply commodities, suppl
commodities at unduly discriminative or unduly low prices, or un
reasonably inducing customers of a competitor to deal with onese
This term is also defined to include methods of competition which
Fair Trade Commission may designate as contrary to the public int
(Lav for Prohibition of Private Monopoly and Preservation of
Fair Trade, No. 54, Gazette No. 309, April 14,.1947; as amond

Law for Exceptions to Law No. 54 (1947) Relating to Prohibi-

Labor Standards Law
This law, vith many facets, provides minimum standards of working
conditions, attempts to cover certain features of fair employment
practices, provides for accident compensation, health and safety stan-
dards and covers a multitude of benefits normally the subject of col
lective bargaining. It even gets involved in descent and distributin
laws by requiring of an employer that upon the death of an employee,
unpaid wages, savings funds and valuables belonging to the"deedent
shall be turned over to a claimant within seven days of his reguest.
It provides for miniinum wages to be established by occupation and
:industry by a governmental agency. It establishes an 8-hour work da
and a 48-hour work week vith stipulated rest periods. It requires
rest day a week. It provides that hours in excess of 8 per day or
per week uay be worked if the Union agrees, but requires overtime 
of 25s for additional hours worked, or for work on rest days. It re-
quires an enployer desiring to work employees ovortime to obtain an
agreement from the Union and to submit a copy of such agreement to the
Administrative office. It also provides for a 25% night shift dif-
Icrential between the hours of 10:00 P.Ii. and 5:00 A.M. This law fur-
ther makes it mandatory that workers employed continuously for one
year must be granted 6 days annual vacation with pay, with one day
extra per year up to 20 days. It limits the hours of vork for women
and minors. It requires that the establishment of training programs
must reccive the approval of an administrative office.
In addition to the foregoing this law, although there is a sepa
rate V'orkmen's Compensation Law, requires an employer to pay various
forms of accident, sickness and death benefits for injuries, sick-
ness or death occurring during the duties of an employee, and in the
event of subcontract work, the original employer is deemed the employ
er for this purpose. It provides for the drafting and publicizing,
Vith the Union's approval, of rules of employment, setting out hours
of duty, vages, rest days, holidays, vacations, retirement benefits,
accident compensation, etc. This act also sets up an inspection or-
ganization and grants to Labor Standards Inspectors the povers of a
judicial police officer.
As is apparent from the foregoing, this law goes far beyond any
-3

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.99)
thing attempted in the United States. It grants by legislative fiat
benefits far in excess of anything normally won by workers after yoe
of collective bargaining. In addition to the items above recited 
this law precludes employers from disnissing employees injured or tal
en ill while on duty, or women becouing pregnant or giving birth to
children during a rest period which the enployer is reguired to grant
for such purpose.
Such provisions as those requiring an employer to pay accrued
vages for emergency use prior to the regular pay day, to pay a pro-
portion of workers' vages during periods of temporary lay-off, to pro-
vide for guaranteed wages under a piece-work systen, to provide up to
20 days' vacation with pay, to provide two 30-minute nursing periods
daily for vomen employees vith babies less than 1 year old (in addi-
tion to regular rest periods), to provide menstrual leaves if request-
ed; those requiring cnployers in certain businesses to shift employees
physically unfit for their work to other work or to shorten their
hours; and those requiring that upon request a pregnant woman employee
shall be transferred to a less strenuous job, all are normally sub-
ject to collcctive bargaining.
(Labor Standards Law, No. 49, Gazette o. 303, April 7, 1947, as
amended by Lav No. 97, Gazette Extra, August 31, 1947.
Sce also Workmen's Accident Compensation Law, No. 50, Gazette
No. 303, Apri1 7, 1947.)
Lay for the Termination of Zaibatsu Family Control
This law has as its objective the eradication of the "personal
tic which has served influentially for the formation and maintenance
of thc Zaibatsu entcrprises", and by such eradication "thereby pro-
moting a sound development of the Japanese economy on the denocratic
basis". The term "Zaibatsu" is defined as groups of individuals who
have been designated under the provisions of the HCLC ordinances or
belong to thc same line of family by blood, marriage or similar re-
lationship. This law requires that any Zaibatsu family member hold-
ing any position as an official in a Zaibatsu or restricted company
shall retire therefrom and, for a period of ten years, be excluded
from the position of an official in such companies.
A "Zaibatsu company" is defined as one whose capital cxceeds
¥10,000,000, 30% or more of which is controlled by any of the Zaibatsu
a company in which more than ¥10,000,000 of its capital stock is.con-
trolled by a Zaibatsu, or a company wich has substantially contribu-
tod to the formation or maintenance of Zaibatsu enterprises or which
has served as an influential agency through which Zaibatsu exercised
its economic controlling influence.
The term "official" is defined as a person holding the position
of director, staff member with executive responsibility, auditor, or
other person who. holds any other position such as advispr, or coun-
elor, who exercises authority or influence commensurate with or sur-
passing that of the positions specified.
The law also applies to so-called appointees of Zaibatsu, mean-
ing officials in Zaibatsu companics whose appointments wero subject
to the control of any of the Zaibatsu or who handled policy matters in
the intcrest of the Zaibatsu. Such appointees are likewise, under the
lav, requircd to retire from their positions and aro excluded from
-4

such positions for ten years. To be oxompted Trom

This liw, while it does provide for appcal to the Prime Minister
in effect, precludes the use, in the readjustment of the Japanese
cconomy, of somo of its most experienced and talented personnel. Wit
out any accusations or trial, persons re removed from their executive
positions solely by reason of their relationship by blood, mcrringe or
otherwise to persons cxercising control of a Zaibatsu company.
(Law for the Termination of Zaibatsu Family Control, No. 2,
Janunry 7, 1948.)

the law are found; but leaving to the courts, exclusively,
the pover to make designations or findings of violctions
It is the opinion of this sub-Committee that the injunction
process is adequate to break up monopolistic practices and
that a reasonable anti-trust law is sufficient for that pur
pose.

(b)
Provide for a claims tribunal for the adjudication of war
loss claims even though, because of Japan's present eco-
nomic condition, the sa isfaction of such claims in cur-
rency may have to be posponed.
(c) Establish a realistic dolar-yen exchange rate.
IV.
Summary
Existing laws affecting busine:s are handicapping the restoration
of Japan's recovery because they hale refised to recognize the price-
profit notive of American investors, because they provide for almost
absolute control of business by governmen- bureaus and boards, and
because they depart fundanentally fron recognized principles of free
enterprise. Such laws can and should be changed and, if necessary,
new lavs should be passed, if American business industry is going to
play its much-needed part in the rehabilitction of the Japanese-eoono-
my.
For the Sub-Committee

AN PBOYER

ECONCHTC PHFORN LAWS ENFORCED HERE CRITICIZED
U. S. Chamber of Comnerce Committee
Calls for  holesele Jovision
By Rutherford Peats
UP Staff Correspondent
An Anerican Chamber of Commeree committee charged that the
Occupation's economic reform laws violate American principles of
free enterprise and called for wholesale 
the anti-
monopoly, deconcertration, aibatsu "purge" and Labor Standards
law.
The Chamber circulat d a report filed by a subcommittee
headed by Jean P. Soyer  Mestinghouse Electric International
Co. and Dudley . y  International Standard Electric Corp.
The American busireesmm charged that the economic reform
laws passed under Occupatin inspiration "provide for almost ab-
solute control of insiness b verrment bureaus and boards and
depart fundamentally n pconized principles of free enterprise.
Such laws can and sho1   ge nd, if necessary, new laws
should be pased, if meri irdustry is going to play its much-
needed part h the rehabilitalion of the Japanese economy" through
investments here.
The committee urged consolidation nd easing of laws designed
to break up "excessive co neentretions of ecenomic power" and anti-
monopoly laws and the witing of  single "reelistic antitrust law
no more drastic than the n'itrnst. laws in the United States."
The Chamber of Commerce group charged that the Cccupation's
Labor Standards Law contained "features rsre radical than anything
yet attempted in the United St-tes" and was "premature and ill-advised."
They said, "Japan's greatest need is a nose-to-the-grindstone
effort. It is not the time to provide benefits normally the fruits
of a healthy econcmy" and c:llective barg-ining, such as vacations,
sick leaves, welfare plans and prohibitions against compulsory over-
time.
The American business group termed "most undemocratic" the law
barring from office former zaibatsu executives and their immediate
families.

RESPECTFULLY ReED
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. President:
sving in mind-the heavy duties and responsibilities of your office, we
e hesitated to write you directly on this matter. However, in our
n, it involves a fundamental aspect of our occupation policies in
8
Japan, and-therefore you might wish to have it called to your personal
attention.
To the best of our kmowledge, we were the first firm to import camere-
style cigarette lighters from Occupied Japan. We have spent a consider-
able amount of money in national advertising and sales promotion, and
have established a substantial market for the product in the United States.
In fact, in order to regain the confidence of the American people im
Japanese merchandise, we even went so far as to guarantee the quality of
the item.
We did all this not only for our own benefit, but because we sincerely
believe in the worthwhile benefits which would accrue to all concermed
through healthy expansion of Japanese-American trade.
We did not expect that we would encounter any monopolistic practices
which would interfere with the development of this business, or that our
source of supply would be cut off in the manner indicated in the attached
photostatic copy of a letter which we have just received from a Japan-
ese erporting firm.
It would appear that the information contained in this letter is substan-
tially correct, since my letters of credit have been permitted to expire
before shipment of merchandise, notwithstanding the fact that I hame a
We cannot understand how, through political pressure or otherwise, pos
arrangement, so diametrically opposed to our concepts of free entefprise,
can be legally or morally sustained, nor can we believe that any policy
ho

would be adopted which discriminates in favor of one American importer

We have been led to believe in the past, and this fact has been indicated
many times, that any importer could purchase freely in Japan, and that

referred to above. We have with great difficulty obtained partial ship-

receive the balance. We feel, we believe with justice, that we should at
least receive the balance of the merchandise due under this contract, and,
either a fair share of future production, or a fair opportunity to bid on
an exclusive basis, if such is to be the policy.
This matter is of urgent consequence to many people. We have hundreds of
dealers and distributors throughout the United States mho are looking to as
to fill their orders, and all concerned will suffer serious financial loss
unless our source of supply is quickly re-established.
We are writing this letter to you, since we believe that you are sincerely
dedicated to the principle that all American citizens are entitled to re-
ceive just and equal treatment from their Government at all times, and that
practices in restraint of trade, which are not permissible under the laws
of the United States, will not be tolerated in Japan either, at least for
the duration of our occupation of that country, and therefore, we respect-
fully request your intervention in this matter.
Very truly yours,

apparently valid contract with the Tokyo Koeki K.K. for further

iar. Breen terms the sales arrangement a monopolistic

individual under any kind of discriminatory arrangement what-
soever."

American Chamber of Commerce 1n Japan"; and no. 340 of May 24, 1949,
"Approval by American Chamber of Commerce in Japan of Report of Sub-
Committee on Business Investmente of the Fair Trade Practices
Committee". Enclosed herewith are five copies or an English trans-
lation of amendments to the Anti-Monopoly Law (Taw Relating to
Prohibition of Private Monopoly and Methods of reserving Fair
C
Trade), no. 54, promulgated April 12, 1947, as passed by the Firth
National Diet. Promulgation of the new act, which has veen delayed
pending the completion of necessary administrative orders, will
probably take place before June 20, according to responsible
officials of the Japanese Government.
It is believed that the revisions answer in large part the
objections raised by the American Chamber of Commerce in Japan in
its report on deterrents to postwar investment (see this Mission's
despatches no. 290 and 340). The core of the revised law lies in
the definition of competition incorporated into Article 2; Artiql
6 covering international contracts, Article l0 covering inter-
corporate holdings of stock, Article 13 covering holdings of stook
by company executives, Article l4 covering holdings of stock by
natural and juridical persons (other than companies) in two or
more companies, Article 15 covering mergers, and Article 16 cov
ing transfers of business and assets are amended to legalise act
which do not
UNCLASSIFDED
AUG 3 1949
FILED
ACTION COPY
RETURN TO DO/R PILES WETHIN 14 DAYS WITA A NOTATION OP AOTION TAKEN.

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 379,
June 13, 1949.
-2-
which do not lessen competition as defined in Article 2. That
definition is sufficiently generous, it is believed, to warrant
the conclusion that the Anti-Monopoly Law as amended will not in
most cases interfere with investments in Japan by foreign companies.
The key definition provides that for purposes of Chapter IV,
"Stock Holdings, Multiple Directorates, Mergers, and Transfers of
Whole Business," two companies are to be considered in competition
when they may, "within the normal scope of their business activities
in Japan, and without undertaking any significant change in their
business facilities or practices in Japan," supply the same or
similar goods or services to the same customers or consumers. It
does not appear likely that rivalries between Japanese and foreign
0. (Ton?
firms, except perhaps in foreign markets, will be considered
"competition" within this definition of the term.
The recent amendments have gone largely unnoticed in the
Japanese press. On June 9, however, the (Tokyo) Jiji Shimpo sum-
marized the provisions of the new law, and, while finding cause for
satisfaction in the relaxation of restrictions on intercorporate
holdings, foreign investment, etc., nevertheless concluded editor-
ially that the revised law 1s short of ideal. Specifically, the
paper noted that international freight rate conferences are still
prohibited (it should be noted, however, that the existence of
such conferences was legalized by the Marine Transportation Law,
as will be noted in another despatch now under preparation), and
concluded that "despite the revisions, the Anti-Monopoly Law still
contains provisions which obstruct Japanese economic independence,
and it must be further amended." No elaboration on the term
"economic independence"was furnished, and no specific suggestioms
for further amendment, other than the legalization of shipping
conferences, were offered.
The present emendments are the result of long conferences
between the Anti-trust and Cartels Section, Economic and Scientific
Section, General Headquarters, SCAP, and the Japanese Fair Trade
Commission. One preliminary draft, dated November 2, 1948, was
forwarded with this Mission's despatch no. 734; a second draft,
dated February 15, 1s enclosed herewith. Although the latter draft
is substantially the same as the finel amendments passed by the Diet,
it is believed that a comparison of the various drafts may be of
7
interest to the Department as an indication of progressive thought
in General Headquarters on the subject.
There follows a summary of significant changes in the law other
than the new definition of competition, as well as comments on
notable variations between the November draft, the February draft,
and the law recently passed by the Diet.
Article 6 has been relaxed by requiring simple registration
K
of international agreements, Advance permission to enter into such
agreements had previously been required. The February draft and
UNCLASSIF IED
the final

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 379,
June 13, 1949.
- 3 -
the final law contain substantially identical provisions, requiring
registration within 30 days, while the November draft provided for
registration within 20 days. The effect of the definition of
competition in Article 2 is to be noted in the provisions of Article
6: agreements with foreign firms regerding sales, distribution,
2
techniques, et cetera, in Japan, would probably not be considered to
restrict competition, in view of the fact that most foreign firms
could not, without "undertaking any significant change in their
business facilities or practices in Japan", supply goods to the
same consumers.
Article 9, prohibiting the establishment of a holding company,
is amended by a more detailed definition of the term "holding
company", and by specific statement of its applicability to foreign
firms. The latter provision is incorporated into both the February
draft and the final text, and thus represents a modification of the
November text.
Article 10, regulating intercompany stock holdings, is amended
to allow corporate holdings of stocks issued by other companies if
the effect of such holdings does not substantially lessen competition.
The most significant change over the November version forwarded with
this Mission's despatch no. 734 again revolves around the definition
of competition in Article 2; as in the case of Article 6, the pro-
visions of the present article would not be likely to apply to
acquisition of securities in Japanese companies by foreign concerns.
A provision in Article 10 of the November draft prohibiting a
company engaged in the buying and selling of goods from acquiring
stock in another company in excess of l0 per cent of the capitaliza-
tion of the former company hes been omitted from the final draft.
The November draft has been further softened in that companies whose
assets exceed 5 million yen need only make semi-annual reports of
stock holdings; foreign companies, regerdless of capitalization,
must file such a report.
Article ll is modified by the recent Diet act to make permission
of the Fair Trade Commission automatic in the case of financial
institutions which acquire stocks for resale, provided that the
company "shall carry out an expeditious resale of said stocks;"
this provision is present in neither of the earlier two drafts. A
provision allowing a financial organization to purohase more than
5 per cent of the stocks of a given company upon permission of the
Fair Trade Commission has been deleted from the later drafts, thus
marking one instance in which the final draft tightened rather than
relaxed earlier proposals.
All three drafts provide for the deletion of Article 12, with
the result that acquisition of debentures is to be treated in the
same manner as acquisition of stocks under Article 10.
Article 13,
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 379,
June 13, 1949.
Article 13, regarding interlocking directorates, is amended
to forbid such directorates only in cases of competing companies;
as in the case of Article 6 and Article 10, the definition of
competition in Article 2 thus becomes all-important. The more
complicated provisions of the November draft, which incorporated
portions of Article 10 into Article 13, have been deleted, as has
the provision reguiring reports on interlocking directorates in
certain cases.
Article 14 similarly relies on the earlier definition of
competition, it being provided that no person, natural or jurid-
ical, (other than a company), may own stocks in two or more companies
when the effect thereof is to lessen competition. The time within
which a report of such holdings must be filed with the Fair Trade
Commission, twenty days from the date of acquisition under the
November draft, has been extended to thirty days. The provisions
of the earlier draft have been somewhat tightened, however, in that
1ndividuals who, upon becoming executives of a company, hold stock
in a competing company must eventually dispose of such stocks.
Article 15 is amended by the recent Diet Act to require filing
with the Fair Trade Commission at least 30 days prior to effecting
a merger; the conditions under which mergers are prohibited are
substantially as stated in the old act, except that it no longer
need be shown that a merger contributes to rationalization of pro-
duction, supply, or management. Earlier drafts had contained
somewhat stricter provisions: the November draft had required.the
advance permission of the Fair Trade Commission if the assets of
either party to a merger exceeded l0 million yen; the February
draft, even more severe, had reduced this limit to 5 million yen.
Article 16 is amended to make the provisions of Article 15
applicable, mutatis mutandis, to transfers of business or assets,
entrustment of management, profit-sharing agreements, or leasing
of all or part of the business of another company; the final draft
passed by the Diet is more specific in its applicability to foreign
firms than were earlier drafts.
A new Article, l7-2, provides in some detail for desist orders
against violators of Articles 10 to 16, inclusive; this provision
was included in a briefer and more summary form in the November
draft, and was omitted entirely from the February draft.
The remainder of the amendments deal with administrative, pro-
cedural, and punitive measures, and do not involve any significant
change in principle. Punitive provisions are somewhat more detailed
than under the old Law, and fines have been revised upward in recog-
nition of the falling value of the yen. Supplementary articles
provide for enforcement of the law from the date of promulgation; for
filing of reports on securities held as of April 1, l949; for penalties
against violaters.
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.96)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 379,
June 13, 1949.
against violators of supplementary provisions or ordinances issued
pursuant thereto, including punishment of corporations for which
individual violators have acted as agents or representatives; and
for continued application of certain penalties or procedures under
the old Law when action was in process or when the violation
occurred prior to promulgation of the new law. Measures to be taken
in the cases of securities held in violation of Article l0 and
Article l4, as of the date of promulgation, are to be provided for
by separate order.

(COPY)
PROPOSED AMENDMENTS TO THE ANTI-MONOPOLY LAW
The Fair Trade Commission
15th February 1949.
Article 2
Paragraph 2 shall be deleted and the following paragraph shall
be substituted therefor:
The term "competition", as used in this law, shall be construed
as referring to situations in which entrepreneurs do or can, within
the reasonable scope of their normal business activity and without
significant change in their productive or marketing facilities or
practices in this country, produce and/or market the same or similar
types of goods or services for or to the same potential buyers.
Article 6
1. No entrepreneur shall participate in an international agree-
ment or an international contract with a foreign entrepreneur or
participate in an agreement or contract on foreign trade with a do-
mestic entrepreneur with regard to any matter which comes under any
one (1) of the Items of Paragraph 1 of Article 4.
2. The provi sions of the preceding paragraph shall not apply
in case the effects of such agreement or contract on competition in
any particular field of international or domestic trade are negligible.
3. Every entrepreneur, participating in an international agree-
ment or an international contract with a foreign entrepreneur, or in
an agreement or contract on foreign entrepreneur, or in an agreement
or contract on foreign trade with a domestic entrepreneur, including
such agreements or contracts provided for in the preceding paragraph,
ahall within thirty (30) days after execution of or commencement of
participating in said agreement or contract, file with the Fair Trade
Commission a copy of said agreement or contract, or in case of an
oral agreement or contract, a statement, setting forth the contents
thereof, together wdth such report as shall be required by the rules
and regulations of the Fair Trade Commission.
4. The provisions of the preceding paragraph shall not apply to
an agreement or contract on a single transaction which provides for
the completion of the delivery of the object or for the performance of
services within a period of one (1) year, or an agreement or contract
providing for the establishment of an agency by grant of power of at-
torney only (any agreement or contract including provisions placing
any restriction on the business activities of the contracting parties
is excluded).

a domestic company in another non-financial (the term financial
shall mean banking, trust, insurance, mutual financing or securities

gaged in competition with it. in Japan;

in writing to the Fair Trade Commission on the 30th day of April

resumption of such commeroe, and furthermore, has ably demonstrated that these
beliefs are well supported in practice by his vigorous actions and great
administrative skill.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
OFFICE OF THE SUPREME COMMANDER
5 June 1949
Dear r. Breen:
The matter to which you have requested attention in your
letter of 24 Yay 1949 has been investigated. As I am most earnestly
concerned to preclude inequities from develcping in the conduct of Japan's
foreign trade, I am glad to be inforned of any situation which might
appear to militate against the promotion and oxpansion of Japanese ex-
ports on an equitable basis.
In the present case the yoci Trading Company have, in
their letter of 11 !ay 1949, misrepresented their position as suppliers
of canera style cigarette lichters. The fact is that as manufacturers'
agent this company has simply arranzed with the ihoto Iite Corporation
of Chicago to sell to that corporation the product of two Japanese manu-
facturers of the type of lighter in question. The Yyoci Trading Company's
claim to be the exporter of all canera style lighters mamufactured in
Japan is entirely without foundation and they have been directed to re-
tract their statement to that effect to whonsoever it has been made.
Actually there are at present thirteen manufacturers of
camera style lighters in Japan. A list of eleven such manufacturers,
other than the two affiliated vith the Yoci Tradin~ Company, was fur-
nished you with an carlier letter of 1 June 19l9 from thc oreign Trade
and Conmerce Division, conoric and Scientific Section, which answered
a separate communication from you on this same subject. It is anticipated
that your shippers will be able to obtain from the sources listed whatever
quantity oflighters you desire.
The reason riven for the failure of the Tokyo Trading Co.,
Ltd. to complete your contract with them is delayed production by the
mamufacturer with which the order was laced. The cause of the delay will
be deternined and corrected since the importance of strict adherence to
delivery commitments mist be fully respected by all those engaged in
producing and selling Japanese exports. You ill be further advised in
this regard in complete detail as soon as the necessary authentic infor-
mation has been secured.

The Acting Political Adviser hagthe honor to refer to
COM
Department's airgram instruction no. 119 of July 20, 1949, re-
FTC
questing information on the progress of the economic deconcen-
TRESA
tration program. Enclosed herewith is a SCAP press release of
FRB
August 3, 1949, headlined "Deconcentration Review Board Completes
Task," and summarizing the work of that Board.
The enclosed press release has been interpreted by the
Japanese press to mean that the deconcentration program has been
C
completed; the Nippon Times, Tokyo, for instance, headlined its
coverage of the release "Deconcentration Work Here Ended, M'Arthur
States." For reasons which will be noted in a despatch now under
preparation in this Mission, such an interpretation of the an-
nouncement is not entirely accurate.
With regard to the enclosed release, the Department's atten-
tion is invited to the fact that the analysis of cases handled
by the Board is obviously inaccurate, since the categories listed
do not add up to a correct total. A more accurate classification
would be as follows: companies required to reorganize, ll; com-
panies required to make lesser adjustments, 6; companies still
under consideration (Japan Express Company, Japan Power Generation
and Transmission Company, and nine power distributing companies,
to which no proposed or final order has been transmitted), 1l;
companies released from designation, 29. This listing of course
does not include cases which were not submitted for the Board's
opinion.

(COPY)
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Information Office
Press Release:
1000
3 August 1949
(FOR RELEASE AT 100O HCURS, TOKYO TIME, AUGUST 3, 1949)
DECONCENTRATION REVIEW BOARD COMPLETES TASK
Announcement today by General MacArthur that the Deconcentration
Review Board has completed its recommendations on.all cases under the
Deconcentration Law requiring its consideration marks the completion
of another major phase of the Occupation mission and the finalization
of a much discussed segment of the headquarters effort to revitalize
and democratize the Japanese economy on a peace-time basis. The
Board has submitted its final report and will be dissolved immediately.
Recruited at General MacArthur's request the group of specialists
which composed the board arrived in Japan on May 4, 1948, under terms
of reference which provided for their review of all reorganization
plans proposed by the Holding Company Liquidation Commission for com-
panies desigrated as excessive concentrations of economic power under
provisions of Japanese law. The board was responsible for submitting
recommendations to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers as to
the probably impact of any reorganizations contemplated upon the
general economy. The board further was authorized to make recommen-
dations on any other phase of deconcentration considered germane to
the implemertation of Department of the Army instructions issued pur-
suant to the Potsdam Declaration and the Post Surrender Directive
for the Occupation and Control of Japan issued as a policy decision
by the Far Eastern Commission on June 19, 1947.
Occupation headquarters emphasized that the basic program for
the deconcentration of operating companies has been completed in a
little more than a year and that the elimination of monopolistic
economic power which dominated prewar Japanese life has been accom-
plished effectively without adverse effect upon and no "fragmentation"
of finance or industry. On the contrary competent observers point
to the 50 percent increase in production along with considerable pro-
gress toward stabilization during the past year as evidence of the
growing health of the Japanese economy.
Nembers of the board which has performed such significant service
are:
Joseph Robinson, chairman of the Robinson Connector Company,
chairman of the Board.
Roy S. Campbell, former president of the New York Shipbuilding

Pursuant to basic directives, initial action to implement the
many sided deconcentration program consisted of dissolving and
broadly dispersing ownership in the former Zaibatsu holding companies
through which a small number of families exercised all pervasive con-
trol over the industry, comnerce and finance of Japan. Holding com-
panies other than the Zaibatsu dominated institutions likewise were
dissolved and reorganized under legislation enacted for that pur-
pose. The numerous Control Associations which had exercised exten-
sive regulatory powers over each industry and artificially restricted
production and free economic activity prior to and during the war
were discontinued.
Approximately 1,200 companies which had been affiliated with
the Zaibatsu chain of influence were designated as "restricted con-
cerns" and subjected to detailed surveillance pursuant to Japanese
ordinances pending their severance of Zaibatsu connections.
Anti-monopoly legislation was enacted by the Japanese legis-
lature in early 1947 and the Zaibatsu Family Control Law was passed
in January l948. By these acticns initiated prior to the establish-
ment of the Deconcentration Review Board the great power of the
political-military-economic combines which had planned and prosecuted
the war was effectively broken and legislation established preventing
the re-creation of similar all-powerful organizations.
Public Law No. 207, passed by the Diet in December 1947 had for
its purpose the elimination of "excessive concentrations of economic
power" held by operating companies in Japan whereby free enterprise
and open competition were restricted. Pursuant to the law certain
"standards" were promulgated which were to be used as guides in de-
termining whether a designate company was, in fact, an excessive
concentration. One of the first activities of the board upon its
entry into active operations was to review the standards and proce-
dures of theHCIC under which companies had been designated to deter-
mine whether legal interpretations had been accurate and whether the
procedures were in accordance with the Allied Powers directives.
Initially 325 operating companies were "designated" for detailed
examination under the prevailing standards to determine whether there
was ground in fact for their being considered excessive concentra-
tions of economic power.

After preliminary examination, 50 companies were removed from
designation by the HCLC as not being excessive concentrations and 107
more were ordered removed upon acceptance of minor non-structural ad-
justments. At intervals thereafter in accordance with four basic
principles recommended by the Deconcentration Review Board lll addi-
tional companies were removed from designation for various reasons.
A total of 57 companies, representing 22 fields of finance, busd-
ness and industry was reviewed by the board in detail with recommen-
dations subsequently forwarded to the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers. Among these organizations were financial institutions, steel
and iron companies, paper mills, heavy electric machinery, electric
apparatus and appliances, beer, glass, communications, petroleum, cot-
ton spinning and textiles, silk, chemicals, shipbuilding, railroad
equipment, tools, hardware, agricultural machinery, flour mills, cement,
food containers, warehousing, fishing, ice, refrigeration, freezing,
gas, dairy, coal and metal mining and refining, motion picture and
theatrical entertainment, express and freight forwarding and electric
power generating and distributing companies.
Of the 57 enterprises investigated, 11 were recommended for struc-
tural reorganization, three were required to dispose of specified plants
or stock holdings, two were referred to the Fair Trade Commission with
specific recommendations regarding their trade practices, two were re-
commended for complete reorganization through industry-wide action, one
was required to divest itself of governmental prerogatives, and 37 were
recommended for removal from the list of designated concerns.
In all instances where total or partial reorganization was in-
volved the companies were permitted to submit their own plans for
meeting the requirements and in all cases it was emphasized that any
modifications in corporate structure were to be made without reducing
output.
During the investigations by the board and by other headquarters
special advisors, Japanese industries were provided with valuable tech-
nical information for improving their techniques and technological
procedures.
